Every public mutation runs inside `git_mutation.repository_mutation`, and none reports a failed git step as success: `commit_pending` raises `GitOperationError(step, detail)` on add/status/commit failure (it is the one operation with no envelope to fail in), and sync/resync/push convert that into the failure result they already return, without fetching or pushing after a failed commit. The unattended pair rides the same lock and the same rules with one of its own: `commit_scoped(workspace, *, branch, relpaths, message)` stages `git add -A -- <paths>` over the named pathspecs and commits with a pathspec (which implies `--only`), so a deleted note is recorded and an unrelated file the user had staged stays staged and uncommitted; it verifies `branch` rather than assuming it, because the caller is about to push that one. `preflight_scoped(config, workspace)` runs the same secret scan (`scan_file_for_secrets`, shared with `LocalSessionManager.preflight`, so there is one set of rules) over the eligible set only and returns `backup_scope.tracked_excluded` as blockers. `commit_pending` and `LocalSessionManager.preflight` — the manual, whole-tree path — are unchanged by either. Every merge of `origin/<branch>` (backup auto-merge, sync pull, resync) is refused first when origin adds a path that exists locally untracked or ignored with different content, because git overwrites ignored files during a merge; the backup push falls back to the diverged backup ref.
  backup_scope.py              The explicit path scope an unattended backup may commit (#685), used by `local_session.commit_scoped`/`preflight_scoped`. An ALLOWLIST, not a denylist, in three kinds of scope base resolved from configuration: an agent root (`agent_root_targets`, so the per-workspace re-rooting needs no second rule) where only `memory-vault/`, `skills/`, `subagents/`, `commands/` and the `AGENTS.md` guide are durable; the configured vault, whose directory name is the operator's choice and whose internal layout is the app's own, so provenance rather than a fixed name list is what makes its contents durable; and `.archived-workspaces/`, a container holding one archived agent root per child. On top of that sits a flat set of refusals that hold at any depth: credentials (`.env`, `.envrc`, `secrets/`), runtime state (`.runtime/`), the mirrors `sync-skills` regenerates (`.claude/`, `.agents/`, `.opencode/`, `opencode.json`), dependency trees, build output and caches; plus two refusals that are narrower than a name, both settled in #734. The derived transcript archive (the shared `Logs/` — under the vault before the re-rooting, the install root after — denied at `config.logs_root`; each workspace's own `<agent root>/Logs` is outside the agent-root allowlist) is denied at its resolved `config.logs_root` rather than at the bare name, so a notes folder the operator called `Logs` inside their own vault is backed up; the denial still wins where the vault *is* the archive, and under an archived agent root — a copy of a workspace as it was, which the resolved location cannot point at — its `Logs/Chats/` transcript subtree is denied by shape. And one file is carved out of `.runtime/` — `ALLOWED_FILES`, the automation store `.runtime/schedules.json`, which every other `.runtime` path still excludes (the credential preflight applies to it like any other eligible path); the scaffolded ignore rules have to agree, which is why `cli._WORKSPACE_GITIGNORE_ENTRIES` writes `**/.runtime/*` plus `!/.runtime/schedules.json` rather than the directory, since git cannot re-include a file inside an ignored directory — the `**` and the root-pinned negation are what keep nested runtime directories (a `client/.runtime/` holding credentials) ignored, which a root-anchored `.runtime/*` would have silently stopped matching. Bare top-level durable roots stay out by design: a root reaches the top of the data root only where the data root is itself an agent root, and the status line says so rather than leaving the owner to find it. `data_root` is `local_session.sync_root` — never the frontend URL, never an agent cwd; a path outside it is ineligible whatever it is called, symlinks resolved. `tracked_excluded` reports what git already tracks outside the scope, which is how a committed `.env` becomes a reported blocker instead of a silent gap (`.gitignore` never un-commits anything, and the manual sync path still stages the whole tree).
  backup_service.py            The unattended five-minute memory backup (#689), and the whole of the backup service: `BACKUP_INTERVAL_S = 300` as a named constant (no env var, no setting), one `BackupService` instance per process built in `main` and published as `app.state.backup_service`, and the `BackupStatus` the API serves. `run_backup` is the single serialized path the five-minute loop and `POST /api/local/backup/run` both call, so there is no second git loop and the two can never interleave: it takes `git_mutation.repository_mutation` + `ensure_mutable` at its own entry, re-reads root/branch/remote at run time (never at process start, which is what let an install configured later never back up anything), runs `local_session.preflight_scoped`, commits through `commit_scoped` only when the scope has eligible changes, and pushes when `git rev-list --count origin/<branch>..HEAD` says something is unpushed — so a clean run creates neither a commit nor a push. A blocker inside the scope (a credential-shaped eligible file) stops the run before anything is staged; a `tracked_excluded` gap does not, because the commit is path-limited and cannot carry it, so it is reported as its own count — `BackupStatus.coverage_gap`, plus the run's `extra`, with a human sentence naming the paths in `reason` — and never as a state: a permanent property of a repository that is also a checkout is not a fault of a run, so `needs_attention` keeps exactly its three documented causes and a landed run through a gap is recorded as the success it is, logged at INFO only on the first sighting and whenever the count moves (#733). An in-scope carve-out file that git itself ignores (`.gitignore`, `.git/info/exclude`, `core.excludesFile`) is reported by the preflight as `ignored_in_scope` and named in the status reason, without counting toward `coverage_gap`. Local-commit success and online-push success are kept apart: a failed push leaves the commit where it is (no reset, no force-push, no discarded work) and reports `offline` (unreachable/timeout) or `needs_attention` (everything else, which is what a rejected credential is), and only a push that landed writes `last_success_at`/`last_success_commit`. A `push_branch` success carrying the diverged-backup marker does count as off-device, but still reports `needs_attention` and names the per-commit backup ref. `backup_loop(stop=…, sleep=…)` is overdue-aware at startup (the first delay is what is left of the current interval, so an asleep machine backs up at once and a restarted engine cannot become a push storm), re-evaluates readiness every tick, gates on `enabled`/`paused`/the `legacy_node_state` verdict, and backs off by the bounded `BACKOFF_MULTIPLIER` (12 → an hour) for a failure that cannot self-heal at the cadence. A gate-refused tick (`paused`) stamps no attempt, which is what makes the first tick after the gate lifts run at once — and because the delay is derived from that stamp, a refused tick owes the loop a full `BACKUP_INTERVAL_S` wait of its own instead of re-entering on a zero delay, or a turned-off or client install would call `run_backup` in a tight spin (each iteration a blocking `_scope_summary` read that forks git). Enable/pause and the last attempt/success live in `ciao/app_settings.py` as operational metadata only — never note contents — and the `origin` URL is reported with its userinfo stripped, because a status payload, a log line and a job-run record all copy that string. It also owns the canonical setup prompt (#695): `setup_context(config)` is the trusted, entirely local fact set a setup needs (the absolute data root with its spaces intact, the scope rendered from `backup_scope` rather than a second list of names, the branch, and whether a repo / a *parent* repo / a remote already exist — the parent case being the one where a remote added here would push a whole checkout), and `render_setup_prompt(config, context=None)` renders ONE prompt from it, so the copy action and the in-app dispatch are the same bytes by construction rather than by review. Completion is not detected by any of that: it is the same per-tick readiness re-derivation, so an external agent's setup is seen with no restart. A guided (in-app) setup additionally arms the service through `begin_guided_setup`, and the armed answer is applied by `_settle_guided_setup` only where readiness has *just* been re-derived as configured — dispatch alone changes nothing, and `_gate_reason` lets an armed setup read past `enabled: false` to make that read without ever staging or pushing on the strength of it. The pause is never read past and never cleared, so a pause taken during a setup is the one that counts. Routes: `GET`/`PATCH /api/local/backup`, `POST /api/local/backup/run`, `GET /api/local/backup/setup-prompt` and `POST /api/local/backup/setup-chat` (see `PWA_API.md` → "Unattended memory backup").
  legacy_node_state.py         Detects a legacy host/client/standby/invalid `node_state.json` raw (never through the removed `NodeStateManager`, which created a host state when the file was absent), so node-mode's removal keeps a fail-closed startup gate that never lets a former client become a writer. Four kinds: `none` (no file — a fresh or already-terminal install, so there is nothing to guard), `host` (`host`/`active`), `client` (`client`/`standby` with a valid `http(s)` `host_url`), and `invalid` (unreadable, not an object, unknown role, or a client with no addressable host). `client` and `invalid` are the kinds that refuse to arm the writers; `host` and `none` arm them. Never writes and never raises, and a leaf module by design — it imports nothing from `ciao`, which is what let it outlive `node_state.py`. `main.py` reads it once at startup (`guard_legacy_writers`) and publishes the verdict as `app.state.legacy_node_state`; both writer sites (the scheduler's `is_node_active` and the backup push) then ask that one boot answer. It is the whole gate now: node mode, the manager that used to supply a second, live term, and every route that could rewrite the role mid-session are gone, so nothing re-reads the file and a refused boot stays refused until it is restarted.
  network_addresses.py         Enumerate the URLs the PWA is reachable at (Settings → Access & devices, this device): LAN interfaces, plus any HTTPS origin `tailscale serve status --json` proxies to the engine port, so a Tailscale Serve name needs no typing.
  rate_limits.py               Classifier for the Claude rate-limit status lines that chat history filters out (`is_rate_limit_telemetry`). Quota snapshots are no longer kept (#3138 C2b).
  insights.py                  Session insights model resolution (`resolve_insights_model`: the chat provider's `provider_insights_models` entry, else the source chat's model — a project-closure pass uses the project's last-active chat; calls without a source chat, such as task resolutions, use the workspace/provider default), the generous `_DEFAULT_TIMEOUT_S` for slow operator-chosen models, and `is_context_overflow`, which the schedule attention classifier uses to tell a deterministic oversized-input rejection from a transient failure.
  fts_search.py                SQLite FTS5 full-text indexing and search for vault and transcripts. The database is owned by ONE install: `get_db_path(runtime_dir)` resolves `<runtime_dir>/vault-fts.db` (the app, CLI, MCP, os-audit and startup indexing all pass `config.state_path.parent`), so a production install and a dev checkout can no longer clear each other's derived index the way the legacy global `~/.ciao/vault-fts.db` allowed; `CIAO_MEMORY_DIR` remains an explicit override for tests and tooling and a shared override is diagnosed in the log. Row keys are paths relative to the INSTALL root (`path_base`), so two agent roots each holding a `memory-vault/` cannot overwrite each other's rows; the stored base is recorded in `search_config` and a change to it drops the index (with a warning) rather than mixing two key formats. The prune is scoped to the subtree being indexed, so indexing one root leaves the others alone — which means a workspace-scoped search must pass `path_prefix` (see `vault_key_prefix`) to stay inside its own root, since isolation is no longer a side effect of the prune deleting everything else. Memory bookkeeping never enters the index (`RESERVED_UNINDEXED_FILES`: the proposals queue, consolidations undo log, curation/weekly-review logs) and any note can opt out with `search: false` frontmatter — the memory system's paperwork must not compete with the memories it manages. A multi-word query ranks the notes holding every word first; when they are fewer than `limit`, the OR query's BM25 order fills the remaining slots (notes already returned are skipped, the scope filter still applies), so a paraphrase returns hits instead of nothing and a knowledge update returns both the old-value and the new-value note even when only one holds every query word. Results therefore need not contain every word, and `rank` compares only within the AND run or within the OR run; and every result set is appended to `.runtime/vault_search_hits.jsonl` (size-capped, best-effort) as the disuse signal memory-audit reads (`retrieved_recently` on stale notes); the append and its cap rotation are one per-log critical section (`keyed_lock`) with an atomic replace, so concurrent searches in the read worker pool cannot drop a record or leave a malformed line. `expand_note` is the scoped evidence drill-down the behavioural-eval recall contracts exercise (the `vault_expand` tool that exposed it to agents was deleted in the CLI-first migration's D-03; an agent now reads the best-matching note its search picked before answering, pinned by task `H11-recall-value-below-snippet`): the snippet the search returns is capped at 32 tokens and keeps only highlighted lines, which is what keeps unrelated private lines out of a recall answer but also cuts away qualifications, negations and the paragraph naming the current value. It widens ONE note the caller's own search already matched — the key must be a live `vault_fts` row under the caller's `path_prefix`, and the resolved file must still sit under the vault root, so a transcript, an opted-out note, a traversal, a symlink out of the vault and another workspace's note are all refused — and returns only the markdown block around each matched line, bounded by `EXPAND_MAX_WINDOWS`/`EXPAND_MAX_LINES`/`EXPAND_MAX_CHARS`, with frontmatter dropped and credential-shaped values redacted. Every heading is a block boundary, so a sibling block's secret is never in the answer; the reference is the path rather than an expiring handle, and the index pass plus the file read happen per call, so a changed note is answered at its current revision. Which lines are widened around is decided by the query's content words only (`_EXPAND_STOPWORDS`): the term list feeds a substring test over the note's lines, so before the filter a "for" or a "the" in a natural-language query anchored a window on an unrelated block, which is neither purpose-driven nor bounded by what was asked. A query left with no content word — or one no line carries — returns `reason: "no_line_match"` plus a single fallback block, which the recall contracts treat as context rather than evidence, so recall abstains instead of answering from it.
  frontmatter.py               Split a note's YAML frontmatter on a line-anchored fence and dump one-line YAML scalars; shared by the readers and writers that used to split on the first `---`.
  entity_types.py              The vault's category registry, additive in #622 and the edit surface since #624: `ciao/stock/entity-types.yaml` merged with `<vault>/entity-types.yaml` by `id` into the `EntityType` list behind `EntityTypeRegistry`'s derived views (`canonical_types`, `aliases`, `dir_type_map`, `entity_folders`, `orphan_dirs`) and behind `GET`/`PATCH /api/memory/entity-types` (`effective_payload`, `parse_payload`, `validate_entries`, `user_entries`, `write_vault_file`, `render_yaml`). THAT SWAP IS DONE: the folder map, the orphan set and type inference each come from a registry passed in or loaded from the vault root the caller already holds (#626 wired the indexer, the linter and the audit; #635 wired `config`, the entity tagger since removed in #723, and `vault_rehome`), and a caller holding no registry still gets the stock views, which `vault_index` reads off the shipped list (`DIR_TYPE_MAP`, `CANONICAL_TYPES` and `TYPE_ALIASES` are derived from `ciao/stock/entity-types.yaml`, not spelled out a second time). The registry is read by the API, by the generated `VOCABULARY.md`, and — since #637 — by the entity-note writer (`memory_proposals.write_entity_note`), which files a new note in its category's folder and stamps that category's `type:`, so a category the owner adds can be written to. That writer takes the folder from the agent vault root — the same root the category editor writes `entity-types.yaml` at — so an owner's category edit and the note routed by it cannot describe different directories, and a custom `type:` is not `unknown_type` to a lint run at that root. The stock list is deliberately short: Person, Project, Place, Idea, Resource, Journal and Note, plus two system types the agent writes itself. `core` (Project, Workspace, Skill proposal: the app hardcodes their folder or writer, so `enabled: false` on them is ignored) and `hidden` (Workspace, Skill proposal: not listed on the Categories page) are set by the shipped file only and never read from a vault file. Categories that used to ship (Product, Feature, Automation, Document, Reference, Content) live in `ciao/stock/retired-entity-types.yaml`; `vault_migration.retain_retired_stock_types` copies one into a vault's own `entity-types.yaml` when a note still carries its `type:` or an alias, once per vault on skill sync (receipt-gated) and from `ciao vault-migrate`. `log`/`project-log` became aliases of `journal`. `<vault>` is the agent vault root (`CiaoConfig.agent_vault_root`, the one that owns `VOCABULARY.md`), passed explicitly because that root and the per-workspace notes root differ before the re-rooting. A vault file that is malformed fails closed to stock (logged, never raised, never half-merged); `builtin` is forced by origin, so a vault-only `id` is always custom, and it is never stored. A submitted row is read exactly like a file row — a partial override of the stock entry with that id — and only rows that differ from the shipped default are written, so the file holds overrides and custom entries and never freezes a default into the vault; `write_vault_file` is atomic (unique temp name, one `os.replace`, mode preserved) because a half-written file fails closed to stock rather than to the previous list, and it clears the load cache so the next read sees it. Loads are cached per vault by the vault file's mtime (`clear_entity_types_cache` is the hook). Two views are not fully derivable and keep a documented stock-only table: `_LEGACY_DIR_TYPE_MAP` (`active`/`completed` -> `project`, which `_workspace_of` membership inference needs) and `_ORPHAN_EXTRA_DIRS` (the lower-case `projects`).
  vault_index.py               Build/query memory-vault/INDEX.md and VOCABULARY.md from frontmatter and relative Markdown links in note bodies; owns the canonical `type:` set and the shared link helpers (`vault_link_ref`, `resolve_vault_link`, `markdown_destination`). A note's id everywhere (maps, comparisons, graph ids, INDEX.md links, API rows) is `Entry.path_key`, the path spelled with `/`. CLI: `ciao vault-index`. `scan_vault` takes an explicit `workspace` stamp and `path_prefix`: after the re-rooting a vault belongs to exactly one workspace and its first path segment is a FOLDER name, so the legacy `_workspace_of` inference reports things like `projects` and any `?workspace=` filter drops nearly everything. Owns `load_vault_ignore`/`VaultIgnore`, the vault's `.ciaoignore` of generated paths, which `scan_vault` skips. `scan_targets` merges the vaults named by `CiaoConfig.vault_scan_targets()` — one shared vault before the re-rooting, one per agent root after — rendering each root's paths under `<name>/memory-vault` so two roots holding a note of the same name do not produce the same node id, and returning a rendered-path -> file map because those paths are no longer a fixed offset from one vault root. One scan per target, so a `related:` link cannot resolve across roots. `write_vocabulary_file` takes the category registry as an optional keyword and prepends `entity_types_section`'s `## Categories` block (the configured categories, from the registry handed in) above the type census (the ones the notes use, from the hardcoded set); the default `None` keeps every existing caller byte-identical, which is what lets the registry's adoption by the consumers land separately. The registry is imported one way only — the renderer takes it as an argument, because `entity_types` must never import this module. `scan_vault` skips Markdown files with neither frontmatter nor an H1 unless `include_unheaded=True`, which the project-move planners in `vault_review` pass so a plain-text note's links are still repointed.
  vault_lint.py                Read-only vault health validation for frontmatter, relative Markdown links, orphans, and duplicate stems. CLI: `ciao vault-lint`. Skips `.ciaoignore` matches as sources; they remain valid link targets.
  vault_links.py               Text-level primitives shared by every module that reads links out of vault markdown: the frontmatter/fence/inline-code/markdown-link/wikilink patterns, `is_escaped`, `is_link_start`, `parse_wikilink`, `alias_separator`. A leaf module by design — it imports nothing from `ciao`, which is what lets `vault_index`, `vault_lint`, `vault_migrate_links`, and `vault_rehome` all depend on it without a cycle. Resolution stays in `vault_index` (`resolve_related`, `resolve_vault_link`, `markdown_destination`); receipts and the vault git rail stay in `vault_migrate_links`.
  vault_migration.py           One-off migration of an existing vault onto the canonical `type:` vocabulary. Applies aliased renames, reports types with no canonical equivalent, and keeps retired stock categories a vault still uses (`retain_retired_stock_types`). CLI: `ciao vault-migrate`. Its receipt is keyed on the VAULT (`vault-vocabulary.<label>-<digest>.json`), not on the runtime root, because one `CIAO_RUNTIME_ROOT` serves every workspace's vault; `read_receipt` with no vault named is the install-wide view, assembled from the keyed receipts and answering None until every vault has one (a pre-keying unkeyed `vault-vocabulary.json` still counts, for a single-vault install only). Every writer has to write the file that view reads — the Home card's run button writes each reported vault's own keyed receipt (#814), because a writer that names no vault wrote a receipt nothing on a re-rooted install could read.
  okf.py                       Export the vault (or one workspace) as a portable Open Knowledge Format bundle: notes plus a bundle-root `index.md` carrying `okf_version`, as one .tar.gz. Refuses a vault still written in wikilinks. CLI: `ciao vault-export`.
  vault_migrate_links.py       One-off conversion of vault `[[wikilinks]]` to relative Markdown links; also detects an unmigrated vault for the `os-audit` notice and the Home card, which share that condition through `ciao/migration_notices.py`, for `ciao vault-lint --migrate-links`, and for the existing-folder onboarding offer (frontmatter `related:` becomes bare refs). Dry-run by default. Applied migrations hold a stable runtime lock and publish a vault-bound write-ahead receipt under `.runtime/migration/vault-links.json` before replacing notes. The schema-3 receipt binds the canonical vault path, the vault directory's inode (`null` when the filesystem reports none) and a link operation id, and its images preserve exact UTF-8 bytes and permit interrupted apply or undo to resume; conflicts and invalid, foreign, replaced or stale evidence refuse safely. The portable `.ciao-vault-links.json` marker (`format`, `operation_id`, `state`) is committed with the vault and shares link state with other runtimes and clones: completion is the runtime's own current receipt, else the marker's `migrated` state. Legacy schema-1 and schema-2 receipts are adopted once on the first read, with their exact original bytes kept at `vault-links-legacy-<sha256>.json` beside the receipt. Stale or replaced evidence never authorizes undo or resume. Re-rooting stashes the marker with the regenerated root notes and relocation leaves it at the shared root. Known limits: a vault replaced before adoption inherits the legacy receipt, inode reuse on ext4 and tmpfs after a delete and re-create is not detected, filesystems without inodes cannot detect replacement, two runtimes working on one vault at the same instant are not serialized, and undo evidence is stranded after another runtime's later migration. Kept limits: schema-1 undo preserves current non-span bytes but cannot recover historic newline bytes it never recorded; descendant path checks reject observed symlinks, not an adversarial swap after the final check; the lock serializes managed callers, not external editors. Refuses a `--vault-root` nested inside the configured vault, preview included: refs resolve against the root it is handed, so a workspace subtree makes every link out to the vault's shared notes look dead. CLI: `ciao vault-migrate-links`, `ciao vault-unmigrate-links`.
  vault_rehome.py              One-off re-homing of person notes a global memory-curation run filed into the wrong workspace: moves the tag-obvious ones (`TAG_WORKSPACE_ROLES` maps a tag to a workspace *role*, bound to a registered name) and repoints every inbound reference in both link dialects plus frontmatter refs; notes with no workspace-naming tag are queued in that workspace's `Workspace/Memory-Proposals.md` and never moved. Planning exposes unreadable note paths as observational `read_errors` without changing apply decisions. Dry-run by default, receipt-gated under `.runtime/migration/vault-rehome.json` with an exact reverse map. CLI: `ciao vault-rehome`, `ciao vault-unrehome`.
  proposal_kinds.py            Sole owner of the proposal-queue kinds (memory, profile, user, rehome, share), the bullet pattern derived from that table, and each kind's accept descriptor. Replaced three divergent private regexes that between them never matched `[rehome]` and left 64 real queue rows uncountable. Rehome and share accepts are structurally distinct from a bounded-region edit; a share requires its attended consent flow.
  proposal_actions.py          The transport-neutral half of resolving one proposal, shared by the PWA routes and the CLI. Two things live here. `ProposalActionResult` is the per-row payload an accept or dismiss returns (region, destination, rehome candidate, usage, error), built once in `build_accept_result` for the single-row and the batch route alike — the two used to hand-build the same dictionary and had already drifted. `record_decision` is the one handler that writes a settled row to `memory_proposals`' decision sidecar (append-time dedupe reads it, so a missing row means the curator re-files the fact); it also carries an optional `reason`, already validated by the caller. Ownership boundary: this module never imports Starlette and takes no `Request` — `routes_api.py` keeps request parsing, status mapping and the 409/503 guards, `ciao/web/proposal_service.py` keeps the queue files and performs the promotions, and `ciao memory-proposal-dismiss` reaches the same recorder without going through the web layer.
  proposal_tracking.py        Stable content-derived proposal IDs shared with the proposals routes, plus a cheap re-scan of pending IDs (no rehome evidence) used by proposal helper chats to verify their target proposal has actually left the queue before auto-archiving.
  proposal_predictions.py     Shadow verdicts for `[memory]`/`[profile]` rows: the append-only `Memory-Proposals.predictions.jsonl` sidecar keyed by (kind, one-line text), the join onto History rows, the agreement rate over the last 50 operator decisions, and the handover gate (`handover_allows`). Keyed by text rather than `stable_proposal_id`, which a duplicate removal renumbers and CLI decisions never record.
    operator_actions.py          Home-screen housekeeping strip: a registry of cheap detectors that turn detected install conditions (update, vault location, retired vocabulary, unmigrated links, missed one-timers) into clearable operator actions. Detection reads receipts, registry values and the vault's link marker only, never walks the vault (the one write a poll can cause is the one-time adoption of a legacy link receipt, under the runtime's `migration/` directory); the one notice that needs a walk (unmigrated links) reads a verdict a bounded off-loop scan stored, so the strip stays cheap and the card is still only drawn when a scan found a note — see `migration_notices.py`; pending proposals are not an action, since Home's *What changed* rail already counts them; the PWA endpoints live in routes_api.py. Actions may also carry an external link (release notes, the GitHub star nudge) and a "Later" dismissal that writes a suppression receipt (`dismiss_action`), so an ask-style action can be snoozed rather than only fixed. A run button re-reads the condition it came from and reports that, never the run's own arithmetic — the vocabulary press re-migrates every vault whose receipt still lists unresolved types, writes one keyed receipt per vault and leaves an already-migrated root untouched, and a root still without a receipt is named in the summary rather than rounded into a clean install (#814). The skill-triage card resolves its sheet through the re-rooting receipt's `created_files` rather than a path of its own, because the sheet lands inside the primary workspace's vault and its leaf is the registry's fact, not this module's; it counted rows whose **Destination** cell is blank, so an answered sheet clears it (#810). The provider-update detector (#1592) reads `DetectionContext.provider_checks` (the cached offline `provider_versions` check, wired as `app.state.provider_checks_fetcher`) and emits one advisory `provider-update:<provider>` tile (severity 40, `blocking=False`) for each provider in the `below_tested` state; the tile carries `terminal_action` and `command` (new `OperatorAction` fields the client renders, #1593), a "Check again" run that invalidates the cache and re-reads, and a "Not now" that writes `<runtime>/provider-update-dismissals.json` (`{provider: tested version}`) and hides the tile only while the tested version equals the dismissed one, so a release tested with a newer version shows it again.
  update_task_catalog.py          Typed catalog of versioned update-task definitions, loaded and validated as a whole. `load_catalog` reads the packaged `ciao/stock/update-tasks/catalog.json` — a list of task objects carrying exactly `id`, `revision`, `since_version`, `scope`, `title`, `why`, `detector`, `completion_check`, `prompt_resource` and `depends_on` — and returns the rows that are sound plus a `Diagnostic` for every problem instead of raising, so a malformed packaged catalog degrades to diagnostics and can never crash a Home render or a startup path, nor be mistaken for "the tasks are done". `DETECTORS`/`COMPLETION_CHECKS` are the registries a row's names must come from, and a registered name with no implementation yet is a `not_implemented` warning rather than a load failure; both registries are populated, because every shipped row names probes that ship with it (`learnings-cleanup`, and `unrehomed-people` since #833) — a row that shipped ahead of its code would be offering work this engine has no way to decide or verify. `eligible(installed_version)` keeps a cumulative catalog cumulative (every task the installed version supports, not only the newest release's) and `parse_version` is `package_version._version_key`'s own ordering applied to a `v`-stripped spelling, with prerelease/dev suffixes and build metadata included, so gating and the update check cannot disagree; a task needing a newer engine is excluded but stays in the catalog, so a downgrade does not lose it. `read_prompt` reads a task's `prompt_resource` path (convention `prompts/<id>-<revision>.md`) through `importlib.resources` rather than a checkout path, confined to the packaged directory and re-checked at the read. Pure: no writes, no vault, no network, no model. Applicability, per-scope state and chat launch are the later children of #729.
  update_task_preview.py        Observational preparation of shipped update tasks using existing planners; read-only previews never authorize an apply or settle tasks.
  update_tasks.py               Durable per-scope state and cheap applicability for "After this update" tasks — the half `update_task_catalog.py` deliberately leaves out (issue #756). **State** is `{"schema": 1, "tasks": {"<id>@<revision>": {...}}}` in two places, chosen by the task's own `scope`: a `workspace` task's decisions live in that vault's `Workspace/Update-Tasks.json` (so a backup or sync of the vault carries the operator's dismissal with it, and that file is in `vault_index.RESERVED_UNINDEXED_FILES` — a guard rather than a fix for a live leak, since every consumer of that set reads markdown only today — so no index or lint consumer can treat it as a note), an `install` task's live in `<runtime>/update-tasks.json` beside the other per-install records. A `TaskState` is `(task_id, revision, scope, lifecycle, updated_at, attempted_fingerprint, evidence, chat_id, prompt_digest)` over the lifecycles `offered`/`in_progress`/`waiting_review`/`failed`/`completed`/`dismissed`; keyed by the exact `(id, revision)`, so a dismissal suppresses that revision only and a material new revision re-offers the task. Writes go through `write_task_state`: read-modify-write under `async_reads.keyed_lock`, a unique temp file plus one `os.replace`, sorted keys so rewriting an unchanged state is byte-identical. Nothing absolute is ever stored — a state file is synced, backed up and read on another machine, so a path-shaped or deeper-than-four-level evidence entry is refused (`UpdateTaskStateError`) rather than repaired. A missing state file is a fresh scope that has decided nothing; a file that is there and unreadable (bad JSON, wrong schema) is never silently treated as empty, and `write_task_state` refuses to build on top of one. **Applicability** is `applicable` / `not_applicable` / `unknown`, and `unknown` is the answer whenever the answer is not known: a detector name with no entry in `DETECTOR_FUNCTIONS`, a detector that raises, a detector that returns something that is not a `Detection`, and a state file that cannot be read. Only a detector that ran and returned evidence can produce `applicable`; nothing here can fabricate one, because offering follow-up work this engine cannot substantiate is the failure that costs the operator's attention, while withholding a task that did apply is recoverable. `record_completion` is the only writer of `completed`, and only through the row's registered `COMPLETION_FUNCTIONS` check — an unregistered, raising or unsatisfied check records nothing, so a chat being opened is never mistaken for the work being done. **Nothing else writes it, and #756 left nothing calling it either**, which is why `evaluate` now settles: for a task whose fresh detector answer is being computed and whose record is in `SETTLING_LIFECYCLES` (`in_progress`/`waiting_review`/`failed` — an undecided attempt, as opposed to `offered` where nothing was started, or `completed`/`dismissed` where the verdict or the operator's decision already stands), it asks the check once and writes what the check says, then re-reads the record so one call reports both the unchanged `applicable` answer and the settled lifecycle. `dismissed` and `completed` are never re-asked — re-asking a verdict on a timer would reopen a decision nobody asked to reopen — and neither is a task with no record, or one in an unreadable scope: the evidence is about the workspace, the attempt is what says the workspace was asked, and a file this install cannot read cannot be written either. The settlement rides the *same* per-task window as the detector rather than adding a second clock, so the bound and the honest answer about how fast the world is looked at stay in one named constant (`APPLICABILITY_TTL_S`); the state file is still re-read on every call, so a settled record is visible on the very next listing. The lifecycle is revalidated **under** the write lock (`record_completion(only_from=…)`), not from the read that chose it: the check runs outside the lock on purpose, so a `dismissed`/`reopened` that arrives while it runs would otherwise be overwritten with `completed` — the lock serialising the writes while the decision came from before the first one. A settlement that loses that race, or whose write cannot land (`UpdateTaskStateError`/`OSError`), is logged and skipped and the record re-read, because this runs on a Home listing: one unwritable state file must neither take the strip down nor be reported as done. This is the difference between a task an operator can finish and one Home asks about forever: `learnings-cleanup` stays `applicable` in a workspace whose review retained rows (the detector is right — those rows are still nobody's to retire), so without a settlement the card never goes away. `evaluate` returns the eligible tasks (`catalog.eligible(installed_version)`) as `TaskStatus`, with `offered` = applicable and not suppressed at this revision, and it runs the whole check through `async_reads.run_read` (bounded, coalesced by scope/version/token, never blocking the loop) and reuses the previous answer for `APPLICABILITY_TTL_S` (300, a named constant — not a setting and not an env var) or immediately when the caller's `change_token` differs, so a detector that walks a large vault runs at most once per fresh window while the small state file is re-read every call and a dismissal never waits out a TTL. A downgrade hides the tasks this version cannot support and keeps their records, so upgrading brings them back. Every registry name has an implementation, because #728-E landed `learnings-cleanup` with both probes behind it and #833 landed the install-scoped `unrehomed-people` re-home the same way; a task whose detector is `not_implemented` must not be offered, and the layer is proved end to end over a test-injected catalog as well as over the shipped one. One rule the second shipped task brought with it: a task may share a **receipt** with a surface that cannot dismiss it, but it need not share its **evidence**. `unrehomed-people` is the same condition the audit reports as `unrehomed_people`, and it is the first pair where the two disagree about what happens next — the card can be dismissed or completed and disappear, the report cannot be silenced at all — so both probes call `migration_notices` rather than restating anything, and nothing in that module reads a task record. What they do not share is the strength of the answer: the audit keeps a cheap broad receipt check, while the detector runs one `plan_rehome` walk to establish a real candidate. **This is the only applicability probe in the catalog that reads the vault**, so the per-task `APPLICABILITY_TTL_S` window, the `run_read` hop and the coalescing key are what make it affordable at all, and the probe front-loads four cheap gates so the common answer costs no vault read. The evidence each probe returns is portable by construction: the re-home completion check carries the receipt's timestamp and two counts and deliberately not its `vault_root`, because a state file travels between machines. No `eval`, no shell, no remote fetch, no model call, and no chat launch — `chat_id` is a field this child always leaves empty for #729-C. No route, so no `PWA_API.md` entry.
  logs_split.py                One-time update migration: moves each registry-resolved Logs/Chats/chat-* folder from the shared <install>/Logs into its workspace's Logs/Chats. Unresolved items stay in the shared root. Receipt .runtime/migration/logs-split.json; re-runs merge idempotently, and a chat with a pending turn journal is skipped until the next boot.
  workspace_reroot.py          Plan and receipt for the per-workspace agent-root migration. Classifies every top-level vault path into moved, kept global, regenerated or ignored, and REFUSES on anything unclassified: an unregistered directory, a loose unrecognised file, a symlink, a registered workspace with no vault, or a non-empty destination. Planning never writes. `read_receipt` gates on `status == "migrated"`, so a rehearsal cannot make the real migration look done. Also rebuilds each root's INDEX.md/VOCABULARY.md with no path prefix and drops and rebuilds the FTS database, since every pre-migration row points at a path under the shared vault. Repoints the workspace registry and records an exact before-image, and `CiaoConfig.agent_root` flips to the per-workspace subdirectory only once a migrated receipt exists, which makes the change atomic per install. Moves the whole skill catalog (`skills/`) to the primary root and writes `Workspace/Skill-Triage.md` with every destination blank, because 16 of 20 skills are scoped to one workspace and copying the catalog into every root would rebuild the global catalog the release ends; `skills-src/` is created as a shared mirror source, not a workspace. `skill_triage_sheet` is how that sheet is found again: it reads the location out of the receipt's own `created_files` rather than recomputing it, matches the sheet on a component boundary rather than on characters (`evilWorkspace/Skill-Triage.md` is a different file), and checks the claim instead of obeying it — relative POSIX, no `.`/`..`, contained under the **resolved** install root so a symlinked component cannot carry it out. Anything else raises rather than reporting no sheet, because a receipt is runtime state this process reads and does not own; `undecided_skill_triage` reads the sheet back and names the rows whose **Destination** cell is still blank — a row too short to hold one counts as blank, since a truncated row cannot be said to be answered — which is the operator's record of a decision outstanding and therefore the card's completion evidence (#810). Splits the shared guide (`AGENTS.md`): the file itself moves to the primary root with `git mv`, so its regions stay byte-identical and history follows, and it must not be left at the install root because providers walk UP from cwd for a guide and a survivor would re-inject the primary's memory regions into every root. Every other root is written the same unbounded body with EMPTY bounded regions, and the primary's region entries are queued into that root's `Workspace/Memory-Proposals.md` as one parseable bullet each — nothing lost, nothing guessed. Then `bootstrap_root` installs each root's agent assets through the same code `--repair` uses, so "after a migration `--repair` is a no-op" is an invariant rather than a coincidence. Finally every open chat holding a provider session is marked `handover_context_pending` and the count reported: a session is keyed by the cwd it started in and the migration changes that cwd, so the next turn starts fresh — carrying a context capsule and saying so beats silently forgetting. Only the ids it set are recorded, so undo clears exactly those and leaves a flag another path set alone. It writes the chat state file directly, so the migration must run with the app stopped; it is moving the vault out from under a running server either way. Nothing seeds a per-root guide while the install root still holds an unsplit one (`guide_split_pending`): the packaged stock guide has empty memory regions, so seeding it there would replace the user's guide and silently drop every remembered fact. `--repair` reconciles an already-migrated install back to the registry and is idempotent: it creates a missing root with its agent assets, renames a surviving legacy `CLAUDE.md` onto the root's `AGENTS.md` (reported as `legacy_guide` drift), re-mirrors packaged, own and shared (`skills-src/`) skills into the root's catalog, rebuilds an `INDEX.md` that is absent or still keys entries under a workspace name, and drops and rebuilds the search index when an indexed path no longer resolves. Three drifts are REPORTED, never guessed, and any of them makes the command exit 1: a root with no vault (which notes belong to a workspace is a question about the user's own material), a `.mcp.json` absent against a shared one (an MCP entry grants credentialed access and per-root composition does not exist yet), and a root with no guide while the install root still holds the pre-migration one — seeding the packaged stock guide there would replace the user's guide with empty memory regions, so the split has to run first. It refuses outright on an install that has not re-rooted: there, a workspace prefix in the shared `INDEX.md` is correct and is what `_entity_visible_in_workspace` filters on, so "repairing" it would leave no filter over the index. `migrate_if_needed(config)` is the unattended entry point: `ciao/main.py` calls it at every start, after the git sync so the clean-tree gate judges the real tree and before the index refresh so the indexes are rebuilt for the layout that now exists, and before the server binds so no chat holds a session in a directory that moves. It never raises — a migration that cannot run is a condition to surface, not a reason to fail an upgrade — and it is receipt-gated, so it is a no-op on every start after the first. When it refuses, `operator_actions._detect_workspace_unmigrated` reads the refusal back out of the receipt (via `peek_receipt`, which unlike `read_receipt` returns a refused one) and raises a `blocking` housekeeping action whose run button retries through the same `migrate_if_needed`, so the button cannot drift from what startup does. Blocking means unmissable and not dismissible, deliberately NOT an app-wide lock: the one realistic refusal is an uncommitted vault, and locking the app would take away the assistant needed to fix it. CLI: `ciao workspace-reroot [--rehearse|--apply|--repair|--undo]`.
  workspace_archive.py         Archive and restore one logical workspace, replacing the old delete that merged a workspace's projects and notes into the primary one. `plan_archive` refuses the primary or last workspace and every shape it cannot move without guessing (a vault outside the install, a workspace that owns the whole shared vault, a legacy vault pinned outside its standard folder, a symlink, another filesystem); `move_to_archive` renames the folder byte for byte into `<install>/.archived-workspaces/<name>-<YYYYMMDD-HHMMSS>/` beside an `archive.json` (registry entry, layout, original path, archived_at, the user schedules that left with it). The folder is the whole agent root `<install>/<name>/` on a per-root install and only `<vault>/<name>/` on a shared (not re-rooted) one, whose guide and skill catalog are shared. After the move the workspace's transcripts that were written to its agent root's `Logs` while the move ran are carried into the archive (`carry_late_transcripts`, so the folder is gone again and a rollback can put it back), the workspace is unregistered, its search rows are dropped (`fts_search.forget_subtree`, and its transcript rows through `forget_transcript_subtree`) and the shared `INDEX.md` is rebuilt on the shared layout. A restore brings its `Logs` back with the folder. `restore_archive` moves it back and re-registers it, refusing a taken name, an occupied folder, or an archive made on the other layout. `archive.json` stays in the git-synced install (ignoring the archive root would sync the moved folder as a deletion), so a restore treats it as untrusted: registry fields are rebuilt with the Settings rules (`restored_settings`, falling back to the narrower default), the vault root must resolve inside the restored folder with no symlink on the way, and schedules are rebuilt by `schedules.restorable_user_schedule`, pinned to the workspace and restored paused. Archive, restore, create and update share one registry lock, and a restore rechecks the name after its off-loop folder move. Routes: `POST /api/workspaces/{name}/archive` (DELETE is an alias), `GET /api/workspaces/archived`, `POST /api/workspaces/archived/restore`.
  workspace_census.py          Read-only survey of a vault root into the shapes a per-workspace migration must handle: notes and non-markdown files per top-level directory, symlinks, max depth, duplicate stems, frontmatter-less notes, and registered vs unregistered directories. Never writes. CLI: `ciao workspace-census`.
  workspace_discovery.py       The bare-shell install discovery (#943, moved out of `install_discovery.py` by #3130): `discover_workspace(base)` answers "which installed engine is this shell talking to?" so `ciao.config.installed_workspace_env` can overlay the install's workspace `.env` instead of manufacturing a bootstrap root beside it. On Linux it reads `CIAO_WORKSPACE` from the root-owned, not group- or world-writable systemd unit (`linux_service.unit_workspace`), skipping a workspace the current user cannot read. When nothing was found on Linux, and on every other platform, it reads the engine LaunchAgent through `macos_service.discover_runtime`, which is the call the existing discovery tests patch. In bundled-app mode it answers before those branches with the app-owned workspace pointer (`app_engine.read_pointer`), mirroring the branch `installed_workspace_env` installs, so a bundled engine never reads the LaunchAgent plist that still names the 1.x workspace before a takeover. Both branch kinds are read-only and mint nothing, and both refuse a definition naming a directory that is no longer there, so a stale install is not recreated. `trusted_registry(name, vault)` is the gate the unattended memory handover reads through: it drops `--workspace`, `CIAO_WORKSPACE` and `CIAO_RUNTIME_ROOT` from the caller's environment, discovers the install, and returns the config only when that install's registry registers `name` at exactly `vault`, so an agent cannot name a registry other than the one it is running on. Tests: `tests/test_workspace_discovery.py`.
  vault_relocate.py            Plan and receipt for moving ONE workspace's vault to its standard folder — the narrower fix for a vault adopted before the per-workspace subfolder convention, distinct from workspace_reroot.py's whole-install migration and touching only that workspace's registry entry. When the vault IS the shared vault root, classifies its top-level entries per workspace (this workspace's own content moves; another workspace's folder and vault-wide shared state — Logs, Templates, generated indexes — are left alone); when it lives elsewhere, moves the whole directory. REFUSES on a symlink or anything else it cannot classify rather than guessing. Every move is a `git mv`, gated on no uncommitted tracked changes, with its own receipt kept separate from workspace_reroot's (so neither's status is mistaken for the other's) enabling an exact `git mv`-reverse undo. Feeds the "vault is not in its standard folder" housekeeping card's `chat_prompt`, so the agent runs it directly instead of re-deriving the move by hand. CLI: `ciao vault-relocate <name> [--apply|--undo]`.
  vault_verification.py        The former post-answer vault verifier is retired; historical verdicts remain readable. Also contains a bounded, typed, read-only vault-recall claim checker (strict findings, bounded evidence reads and one fenced helper call). The managed model-invoked operation is enabled (`MANAGED_RECALL_CHECK_ENABLED = True`) and prompted as a best-effort internal check before answering; it is not a hard pre-final interception, so invocation and temporary-file cleanup are not guaranteed. Prompt guidance does not enforce that providers avoid retaining claim text in private traces. Shared process-local admission permits at most two unfinished checker tasks and twelve admissions per trailing sixty seconds. Aggregate busy/rate-limit refusals refund the provisional pass reservation; immediate authorization failures remain charged. The counters reset on restart and are not a monetary or token-spend guarantee. The operation binds to the exact registry session selected by the authenticated `AccessToken`; revocation, chat revocation, scope rotation and expiry invalidate that handle. A request-owned 250 ms polling watcher provides cooperative detection during waits, with synchronous checks at admission, immediately before helper execution and at final delivery; this is not a hard cancellation guarantee. Results return only to the HTTP requester (normally the model's ordinary tool result), after timeout-context exit, owned-task settlement, scalar scope/routing revalidation and strict JSON/UTF-8 preflight. The existing chat token does not authenticate an originating turn or process. A still-valid same-scope token retained from an earlier pass is indistinguishable in a later pass and may consume that pass's free attempt. One attempt is allowed per provider pass, helper routing uses the configured helper resolver, and `verify_vault_answers` remains an independent opt-out. A disconnect alone need not cancel the handler, and opt-out after evidence work begins cannot retract evidence already sent. The checker emits no verification-specific assistant message, notification, persisted verdict, or WebSocket event. The former post-answer invocation is retired; the badge and Settings UI are removed, while historical verdict data are preserved. Normal tool transcripts may contain inputs/results; this is not an invisible or zero-retention path. The strict nested JSON/UTF-8 boundary and pre-`_invoke` exception sanitizer apply only here. Its path policy and the CLI reader's resolve/stat/open/fstat checks assume an ordinary non-hostile concurrently-mutating filesystem: there is a TOCTOU window, no atomic snapshot or descriptor-walk confinement, and a replacement FIFO may block before the opened-handle check. Aggregate concurrency/admission controls remain in force for the enabled operation.
  workspace_move.py            Moves the whole install workspace (`CIAO_WORKSPACE`) to another folder on the same disk and repoints the engine's service. Editing `CIAO_WORKSPACE` in `.env` cannot do it: the engine finds `.env` inside the folder the LaunchAgent names. `plan` is read-only and refuses a non-empty or nested destination, another disk (the move must be a rename, never a copy), a TCC-protected source or destination, a service registered for another folder or none, an engine Python inside the workspace, a running engine update or move, and, on Linux, anyone but root or a folder the unit's account cannot enter; it warns about what it does not repair (`.venv`, git worktrees, paths typed into automation prompts). A TCC-protected source or destination is refused only when the move cannot run under the host: on macOS with a verified `Ciaobot Server` host (protocol >= 2) the move job runs as the host's `run-move` child, so the grant the user gave the host covers the move and the plan returns conditional System Settings guidance rather than promising an interactive macOS prompt (#1658); without a host the job stays a bare interpreter and the refusal stands. `start` records a `MoveOperation` beside the update state (outside every workspace, so it survives the move and Settings can report the outcome after the reload) and starts a one-shot sibling job (`com.ciao.workspace-move`) running `python -I -m ciao.workspace_move run`, or on macOS the verified host's `run-move --python <interpreter> --operation <id> --state-dir <dir>` (the argv is rendered from the verified `HostOwnership`, never from a raw path). `run_move` drains through the loopback `/api/admin/drain` (each drain poll and cancel waits up to 30 s, and a request that times out, is refused or cannot connect fails the move with a reason naming the step; a cancel that does not land is appended to it), stops the engine through the update host, renames the folder, rewrites absolute paths under the old root (`.env` values, the `.runtime` state JSON and migration receipts, re-keying the vault-hash-named receipts, absolute symlinks), copies Claude Code's `~/.claude/projects/<slug>` session stores for every agent root and the `~/.claude.json` per-folder entries so open chats resume, rewrites the service definition, starts the engine and waits for `overall_ready`; any failure after the rename restores the definition, reverses the rewrites, renames back and restarts at the old folder, and a `PermissionError` is recorded through `ciao.workspace_access.record_denial` and reported with the move's folder instead of a traceback. On Linux the service account cannot stop its own root-owned unit, so the administrator runs the CLI as root and the move runs in that foreground process (`LinuxEngineHost`: `systemctl stop/start`; `LinuxServiceDefinition`: rewrite the unit, `daemon-reload`), keeping every rewritten or copied file owned by the account it belonged to; Settings shows the `sudo` command (`admin_only`) instead of a Move button. There is no undo verb: moving back is the same operation. Routes: `GET/POST /api/workspace-move`, `GET /api/workspace-move/dirs`, `POST /api/workspace-move/plan`, all but the status read restricted to a loopback peer on top of the session. CLI: `ciao workspace-move <target> [--apply]`. In app mode (Ciaobot for Mac) the service definition and the launchd job do not exist: `registered_workspace` reads the app-owned pointer, `plan`'s `app_mode` warns instead of refusing a TCC-protected folder (a denial is probed before the rename and undone) and refuses a missing pointer or a stopped engine with the app named as the fix, `plan_for` refuses while a takeover journal is open, and `start` refuses outright — the move itself belongs to `workspace_move_app.py`, and the POST route records `app: true` and drains on the engine instead of spawning any job.
  workspace_move_app.py        The app-mode side of the workspace move: the engine inside Ciaobot for Mac has no LaunchAgent and owns no detached job, so the move rides the exec restart `ciao.cli._run_server` already performs on `RESTART_EXIT_CODE`. `before_boot` runs at the top of every `--app-engine` start, before any configuration is loaded, with no server, threads or locks of its own, and reads the app state dir's own `workspace-move/operation.json` (`MoveOperation.app` gates it, `MoveOperation.runtime_root` records the pointer's runtime root before the move): with the folder still untouched (`queued`/`draining`/a stale `stopping`) it just marks the record `failed`; with `stopping` it performs the move itself — probe the destination, rename, rewrite every absolute path (`workspace_move`'s rewriters), repoint the app-owned workspace pointer only after the rename and every rewrite succeeded, phase `verifying` so the booted engine confirms readiness; with any in-flight phase (`moving`, `rewriting`, `repointing`, `verifying`, `rolling_back` — the app or the Mac died mid-move, or ready never came) it rolls the move back and points the pointer at whichever folder actually exists, preferring the source, so a crash loop on the new folder self-heals on the supervisor's first retry and the engine always boots a real workspace, `rollback_failed` at worst, never the setup wizard. The record is read as strictly as the pointer (own `0o600` file, no symlink, bounded) and validated before anything moves: absolute resolved paths, the source is the pointer's workspace, the folders do not overlap, the target is not a symlink and is absent or an empty directory with an existing parent (a missing parent is refused, never created; a rename across volumes records `failed`, never copies); a refused record becomes `failed` (`rollback_failed` for a recovery phase) and nothing is renamed. Record writes after the rename are best-effort and never gate the rename back; the pointer follows the disk and is repaired from a `finally`: it names whichever folder exists and holds the data, and a rollback with both folders populated fails as `rollback_failed` rather than choosing. An engine is never moved under: a held `server.lock` (either folder or the record's runtime root) skips, and a `verifying` record younger than `VERIFY_GRACE_S` (ready timeout plus a minute) is left alone, since a starting engine holds no lock yet; an empty, unparsable or future stamp reads as young, and the record file's mtime bounds the stamp. The skip paths and an unreadable record still repair a pointer that names a vanished folder, under `move.lock`; a boot that finds `move.lock` held waits a bounded time, then touches nothing. S3b-1 is inert on its own (nothing writes an `app=true` record until S3b-2's route does), and S3b-2 MUST write `done` once the booted engine is ready (`verify_after_boot`); until it lands, an ordinary restart more than the grace after a move rolls that move back. Both the move and every rollback run with SIGTERM/SIGINT/SIGHUP ignored (only from the main thread) (restored after; `before_boot` never execs), never raise (any exception is one stderr line, boot continues), and refuse to touch a folder whose `server.lock` another engine holds. It imports only `app_engine`, `workspace_move`, `os_support`, `workspace_access` and `package_version` — never `server_host`, `update_host` or `macos_service`: in app mode the pointer is the only workspace source, the LaunchAgent plist is never read or written, and `cli._run_server` drops an exported `CIAO_WORKSPACE`/`CIAO_RUNTIME_ROOT` before the exec so the fresh process cannot pin the vanished pre-move folder. The request side lives here too: `record` (the move route takes `move.lock` and writes the `app: true` record — the only such writer), `drain_then_restart` (drains chats and scheduled work, writes `stopping`, requests the engine's own restart; the lock's descriptor dies with that exec and the failure paths reopen admission and fail the record), and `verify_after_boot` (the booted engine writes `done` once `overall_ready` is up — never before — else `rolling_back` plus the restart whose pre-boot pass rolls the move back).
  workspace_rename.py          Rename one logical workspace (`old` → `new`) and every reference to it: the registry key, the agent-root directory on a re-rooted install (plus a relative `vault_root` whose first segment is the old name, and background-run `cwd`s under the old root), and the `workspace` field in projects, user schedules, system-schedule overlays (re-keyed `<base>@<new>`), webhook triggers (workspace plus a revision bump, verifiers kept) and import batches (workspace only, destination untouched). Refuses an invalid, clashing (case-insensitive, including archived names) or unchanged name, a busy chat, or a queued/running background run before writing anything; refuses a symlink source, an occupied destination, or a cross-filesystem move. The directory moves before anything is persisted, and a later failure moves it back and restores the registry entry. Reached by `PATCH /api/workspaces/{name}` with a different `name` in the body.
  os_audit.py                  Strict AI OS health audit covering required roots, vault hygiene, skill budgets, rule clashes, bounded memory, proposals, and failed jobs. Split by `scope`: `workspace` reports only the sections describing one agent root, `global` only the ones whose subject is the shared runtime directory (background automation, upgrade actions), `all` both. A section outside the scope is absent rather than zeroed, because an empty section reads as "checked and clean". A named workspace narrows the per-root sections to that workspace's own notes, skills, guide and queue through `agent_root` / `workspace_vault_root`. CLI: `ciao os-audit [--scope all|workspace|global]`.
  memory_audit.py              Model-free rot detection for the always-loaded bounded-memory regions: entries recording a chat event instead of current state, entries citing a path that no longer exists, and one subject carrying two values. Also ages vault notes against one 30-day review horizon (`NOTE_REVIEW_DAYS`; frontmatter `updated:` or mtime; logs/journals and Workspace queues exempt) and region entries against their temporal stamps (`[as-of:]` world-time facts at 90 days, trailing learned-at `[YYYY-MM-DD]` system-time stamps at 180) — informational findings consumed by os_audit.py's memory section, the Memory Map's needs-review list, and the daily curation routine. CLI: `ciao memory-audit` (`--with-vault` adds the aging pass). `find_stale_notes` and `find_stale_entries` are SIBLINGS rather than a detector and a special case, and `note_entry_coverage` is the single per-note core both age through — so a surface cannot show a note fresh and its oldest bullet overdue. The entry detector selects only OLD and UNDATED entries: a valid `[verified:]` stamp inside the horizon is never a finding whatever the note's own `updated:` says, a stamp the parser cannot read (malformed, an impossible day, a day that has not come) is `unverified` rather than fresh whatever the note says, while a *missing* stamp is a finding only once the horizon has passed — a bullet with no stamp inherits the note's date and is current exactly as far as the note is, and selecting those unconditionally made every bullet written before stamps existed read as never checked, filling a nightly plan with one-day-old entries in any vault that had been re-stamped. Each finding says which of the three it is and whether the age is the entry's own or inherited from the note — "unverified for 400d" about a bullet nobody ever stamped is a lie dressed as a number. Exemptions are explicit and narrow: an entry under a heading that says `## Events`/`## History`/`## Log`/`## Journal`/`## Changelog`, an entry matching this module's existing chat-event patterns, and a *leading* date beside a completed-past-tense verb with no present-tense state in it. A bare calendar date exempts nothing, because a lease that ends on the 30th is a current-state assertion that happens to carry one. What the parse did NOT read is counted, not swallowed: a paragraph, a table, a quote, a fence or a bullet carrying one is `uncovered` and never verified, while headings, rules, blank lines and frontmatter are not — which is what makes `fully_verified` a checkable claim (a note is fully verified only when every in-scope assertion is covered, so one fresh bullet cannot badge a note whose second bullet is two years old) rather than a decoration. `note_entries.frontmatter_span` is read for that classification rather than re-derived, because a second frontmatter rule is how the first entry and the frontmatter end come to disagree. Consumed by `curation_run._due_entries` (which now holds no rule of its own), `os_audit`'s memory section, the Memory Map's per-node `entry_coverage` and `vault_review`'s `unverified_entries` signal and `entry_verification` evidence block, so the nightly plan, the audit, the map and the queue cannot disagree about which fact is due — and one fresh bullet never clears a stale sibling in any of them. The two consumers that hold check state (`_stale_entry_items` and `vault_review._entry_evidence`) filter it through the SAME `entry_verification.check_settles_entry`, so a fact somebody has already been asked about is not offered twice; `curation_run._stale_findings` also returns notes it could not date, flagged `dated=False`, because an entry carrying its own stamp is aged from that stamp and the note's date is only the fallback.
  note_entries.py              The per-entry parser and identity model for the facts a note asserts (#736, pure and leaf: stdlib only, no `ciao` imports, no disk, no model). `parse_note_entries` reads a note's text into `EntryDocument`: an entry is one Markdown list item plus its more-indented continuation lines, a nested child item is its own entry, and headings, rules, blockquotes, tables, frontmatter and fenced code are not entries — ``` / ~~~ fences, info strings, inline code, a fence nested at a list item's content column, CRLF and a BOM are all handled, and `original` plus every offset index the input byte for byte. `uncovered` is the exact complement of the entries, so a caller can report what it did not read as honestly as what it did. A *trailing* `[verified: YYYY-MM-DD]` on the opening line is parsed strictly and kept distinct from `[as-of:]` and the bounded regions' learned-at `[YYYY-MM-DD]`; a mid-line mention is prose, and malformed, impossible, future and duplicated values are diagnostics with `valid=False` (or the duplicate code), never guesses. The fingerprint is sha256 over the entry's exact text with that one stamp token removed (a re-stamp is not a new fact, a prose edit is), and `entry_identity` digests workspace, note path, nearest preceding heading, fingerprint and a duplicate ordinal — no line number, so moving a fact keeps its identity while identical twins stay distinct. `refresh_fingerprint(text)` recomputes from edited text. Two further readers are public because a consumer needs the parser's own answer rather than a second copy of it: `frontmatter_span(text)` bounds a note's YAML frontmatter (a consumer reporting *coverage* has to tell a note's structure apart from its unparsed assertions, and a second frontmatter rule is how the frontmatter end and the first entry come to disagree), and `invalidate_stale_stamps(before, after)` is the rule an ordinary save uses, and it has two halves: a bullet whose words changed, or which is new, has never been verified and loses its stamp; a bullet whose words are **unchanged** and whose stamp differs has that stamp **restored to the token the old file carried** — because the fingerprint ignores the stamp by design, so "the words are the same" cannot also mean "the stamp is fine". That second half is the "a whole-file rewrite carried today's date onto a bullet" failure, and a fingerprint-only rule cannot see it at all. An untouched neighbour and a reordering survive both halves; a changed bullet comes back as `unverified` on the next scan. It is deliberately not used by `note_receipts.apply_entry_edit` or the managed verification, which write a new fingerprint carrying a stamp on purpose.
  entry_verification.py        One *entry's* verification: the agent's outcome for a single Markdown list item inside one note (`still_valid`/`update`/`retire`/`unverified`, #726-B's vocabulary imported rather than restated), the evidence behind it, and the durable per-entry check state that stops the same fact being asked twice (#780). It exists because two of the whole-note path's properties are wrong at this width: a note-keyed check cannot hold two verdicts about two facts, and rewriting a whole note to correct one bullet rewrites every other bullet with it. The key is `note_entries.entry_identity` — workspace, note path, nearest heading, fingerprint, duplicate ordinal, and deliberately no line number and no offset — so a verdict survives a note being reordered and a fact inserted above it, while a re-worded entry is a fact nobody verified and is checked again. The autonomy rule is 726-B's, checked by the same predicates (complete coverage, at least one citation naming the note, EVERY row a citation, the caller's replacement verbatim, never an empty one) and an entry carrying no `[verified:]` stamp gets one on that same evidence (an unreadable stamp is still `needs_review`, since replacing it decides what the author meant), since the token IS the claim and the evidence is what makes it a check rather than an invention. `still_valid` re-stamps (or first stamps) that entry's own date and nothing else, so the fingerprint — which ignores the stamp token by construction — is unchanged and a verified fact stops being work immediately; `retire` is never applied here. The only mutation is `note_receipts.apply_entry_edit`, a splice of the exact entry span, so the undo stays whole-note and exact; the module imports no delete, trash or archive primitive at all. State lives in the SAME `<vault>/Workspace/Note-Checks.json`, in a second `entries` map under the same unchanged `schema: 1` (a bump would make every note's cooldown unreadable to a version that has the note map and not the entry one), and both maps are written through `note_verification.update_check_state` so neither can drop the other's rows. `should_check_entry` is False only while a check for the same fingerprint is inside its cooldown or waiting on a proposal. Tests: `tests/test_entry_verification.py`, with `tests/test_note_receipts.py` and `tests/test_note_edit_proposals.py` as read-only regression coverage.
  trajectory_builder.py        Parse filtered session JSONL into a structured trajectory (turns, tools, skills, errors) and persist to ~/.ciao/trajectories/YYYY-MM/<session-id>.json. CLI: `python3 -m ciao.trajectory_builder --list [...]`.
  cli_envelopes.py             Sole owner of the vocabulary the Claude Code CLI uses for the user-role records the human never typed: the envelope tag table and its start-anchored regex, the `<task-notification>` grammar, the /model and /mode echo prefixes, the "No response requested." sentinel and the interrupt marker. `ciao/web/transcript_service.py`'s renderer skips exactly the records this module recognises, so a record the CLI wrote for itself never renders as a user bubble. The predicates used to be near-copies kept in sync by a comment, and the task-notification pattern had already drifted (one anchored to the whole record and greedy, the other unanchored and non-greedy). The unified rule has two stages, and both matter. First the record must *open* with `<task-notification>` — a completion is a record the CLI wrote as an envelope, so text that merely carries the grammar is not one. A human message quoting a notification, or shell output from a command that printed a session JSONL, is shown as it was typed. Only then is the body searched, unanchored and non-greedy, so a notification with anything appended after its closing tag is still a notification and the first one is the one read. `envelope_notification_fields` is the supported entry point for both stages; what it then reads out of a notification has known limits, named in #502. It lives one level up from `ciao/web/` so non-web modules can import it without importing the web package, the same reason `proposal_actions.py` sits here.
  workspace_guide.py           Sole owner of the workspace guide's filename and the migration off `CLAUDE.md`. The guide is `AGENTS.md`: Claude Code read only `CLAUDE.md` until 2.1.277, opencode has always read `AGENTS.md`, and Ciaobot bridged that by writing the former and symlinking the latter at it. 2.1.277's default instruction-file mode is `claude-md-or-agents-md` — *a project with no CLAUDE.md of its own gets its AGENTS.md files instead, loaded exactly where and how CLAUDE.md would be* — and that qualifier is the whole point: the fallback only engages when `CLAUDE.md` is absent, so the old name has to go for the new one to take effect. Nothing creates or requires `CLAUDE.md` any more, and there is no opt-out. `guide_path` resolves a root's guide, preferring `AGENTS.md` and falling back to a legacy `CLAUDE.md` so the bounded memory regions stay readable between an upgrade and the rename. `migrate_root` only ever *renames* — never copies, never creates — because the guide carries the `ciao:memory` and `ciao:profile` regions and a migration that writes a fresh file discards everything the agent has remembered; a hand-authored `AGENTS.md` beside a real `CLAUDE.md` is merged with the original kept as `AGENTS.md.bak`. `ciao/main.py` runs it at every start, before the re-root so the guide has its final name when the re-root moves and splits it, and it never raises: a guide it cannot move is still served by the legacy fallback. The trade is deliberate — a Claude Code older than 2.1.277 stops seeing the guide.
  subagent_loader.py           Subagent package loader supporting .md and folder-based packages.
  skill_proposals.py           Sole owner of the skill-proposal queue: the `SkillProposal` record and its `SkillEvidence`, the id derived from `(workspace, skill)` alone, parsing and rendering (legacy loose files included), the atomic merged write, the settlement, and the one enumeration the review listing and the helper-chat archive check share. A record is keyed by the skill, so a pass updates one row per skill instead of forking a dated file per run; a re-run over the same sessions renders identical bytes and writes nothing. Settlement is a recorded decision, not a deletion: the decision goes to the workspace's existing Memory-Proposals.md sidecar keyed by a synthetic `skill:<name>` text, then the record's lifecycle flips, so a dismissed proposal stays readable, keeps collecting evidence, and does not reappear in the queue. Ordinary proposals scope that decision to a whitespace-normalized hash of the proposed change, while keeping the one stable queue file per skill; distinct changes reopen, and prior findings remain suppressed after the file is replaced or deleted. Readable legacy settled rows are anchored to their current change without rewriting history. A legacy sidecar whose queue file is already missing remains a skill-wide suppression because its original change cannot be recovered; before a replacement row is written, the existing sidecar records this as `legacy:unresolved`, so later filings cannot mistake that replacement for the original. Overwritten findings also cannot be classified retroactively. Learning-origin rows remain scoped to their own finding. Writes take the existing `memory_receipts.queue_lock` and `write_queue_atomically`; there is no second queue location and no database. Its writer is reachable from a shell as `ciao skill-proposal-add NAME --input-file FILE` (settling is `ciao skill-proposal-remove`): a hand-authored and a pass-authored finding go through the same `upsert_proposal`, and the target is resolved through `skills_inventory.resolve_owned_skill` rather than asserted, so the record's `canonical_path`/`reviewed_revision` are the resolved source's own answers and a stock copy, a provider mirror, a shared source and an unknown name are all refused. The finding is read from a file because every field in it is conversation prose. The memory pass is the only producer; the weekly skill-evolution pass that used to mine trajectories and draft its own edits was retired in #697, along with its `system-skill-evolution` schedule.
