Refused without exception: pending/implementing/interrupted/failed findings, entries no proposal links, entries held back by an unattributable finding, entries whose own line moved, and entries whose only destination was an upstream issue — a filed issue is somebody being told, a rejected draft is a person's answer and does settle it. A `## Promoted / Resolved` entry is not reconciled at all, and a line the parser cannot read is reported and left exactly as written. A new sibling of `learnings_migrate.py`, not a mode of it: two writers on one file, one reachable from an unattended pass, cannot be a re-skinning. `Learnings.md` is bookkeeping, so this is deliberately not a `commit_note_change`. Two write modes, because they are two decisions: `--apply --approval-file` is the attended workflow for the rows a fold cannot answer, and `--apply-settled` is the unattended one the nightly worklist item names by name — `actor="system"`, no approval file, no `reapprove`, and the same `LEARNINGS_CLEANUP_MAX_ITEMS` the pass's budget gives it, capped on the plan as well as the apply so the table cannot print `REMOVE` beside a row the run will not remove. CLI: `ciao learnings-cleanup` (dry-run, `--apply --approval-file`, `--apply-settled`, `--revert`), reused as the `learnings-cleanup` "After this update" task whose completion check requires an **attended** receipt — one written by `--apply --approval-file`, never by the unattended `--apply-settled` run — naming the revision the document has *now*: the one the write left, or, for a review that removed nothing, the one it read. A generated table, a pending approval, a flag's own receipt or a receipt persisted before a write that never landed therefore does not complete it, and a reviewed no-op does.
  project_doc_update.py        Canonical project doc and people-note folds on the accept path (`update_project_doc`, `fold_fact_into_person_note`): the material parts of a conversation — Decisions and Open loops — folded into the project's own doc, or one approved fact merged into an existing `People/<Name>.md`.
  public_release.py            Public extraction allowlist, export copier, and private-data preflight scanner. CLI: `ciao public-preflight export <src> <dest>` then `ciao public-preflight scan <export-root> --private-patterns <file>`.
  release_manifest.py          Signed engine release manifest: builds the versioned artifact list (sha256/size of the engine wheel alone) in publish.yml, verifies minisign signatures against the embedded release key (the only copy of that key left in the repo, since the app and its native verifier are gone), stamps the release wheel's version and sha256 into the Linux installer, and checks packaged PWA assets. CLI: `python -m ciao.release_manifest build|verify|check-static|stamp-installer`; `verify --asset` checks release files against their signed entries.
  release.py                   Release preparation: sync-bump versions across pyproject.toml, ciao/__init__.py, uv.lock, web/package.json, and package-lock.json; align and track the editable root in uv.lock (including under `--skip-dep-check`); report dependency updates; records the tested OpenCode and Claude CLI versions into `ciao/stock/provider-versions.json` (`--untested-provider`, `--skip-provider-versions`, `--refresh-provider-versions`). CLI: `python -m ciao.release <version>`.
  provider_versions.py         Schema, strict parser and loader for `ciao/stock/provider-versions.json`, the record of OpenCode and Claude CLI versions a release was tested with (shipped in the wheel, written by `ciao/release.py`). Also the runtime `release_check` / cached checks feeding Settings, the host-terminal update actions and the Home strip: offline, from the record; suggestions are suppressed when the running Ciaobot is not the record's `ciaobot` release (dev builds), and `current_checks` is the only part that probes a binary (`--version`). The develop seed records both providers as `untested` / `not_recorded`.
  engine_update.py             The receiving half of the 1.x → 2.x engine update hop (#3130 P6): the detached `run-apply` and `run-recover` jobs, the durable operation record, the update lock, startup recovery of an interrupted swap (`recover_interrupted_apply`), rollback, and `status`. Staging and applying an update are gone: macOS updates through Ciaobot for Mac, Linux through the installer. `tests/test_engine_update_hop_contract.py` pins the verbs and the record fields the old app reads.
  adapter_install.py           The pinned ACP adapters (`ADAPTERS`: `claude-agent-acp` and `codex-acp`), installed on demand into a private folder outside the app (`~/Library/Application Support/Ciaobot/adapters` on macOS, beside the install receipt elsewhere), one `<name>/` folder per adapter, `npm ci --omit=optional --ignore-scripts` against the lockfile in `ciao/stock/adapters/<name>/`, never bundled. A marker (`.ciao-<name>.ok`) names the pin and the lockfile hash; a tree without it is not ready. Each install and launch holds a per-adapter file lock; the module is also `python -m ciao.adapter_install <name>`, the Settings terminal line. `detect(name)` returns the first failing check (`cli_missing`, `node_missing`, `node_old`, `adapter_missing`, `adapter_outdated`) or `ready`, from package.json and the resolved `node`, with no spawn but node's version. `launch_argv(name)` is `[node, <entry>]` for a ready adapter and raises `AdapterNotReady` otherwise, so the ACP connection never starts an adapter whose CLI or node is missing. A machine-wide adapter at the pin, found on the PATH with a real path outside world-writable folders, is used with no install (`in_use_path`). `start_install_in_background` starts the install once per process on a daemon thread, only for a present CLI with a missing or outdated adapter, and returns at once so startup does not wait on npm; a redacted failure is kept for the Settings card, and the card's Install button runs `install_command` in a terminal. The install runs `npm ci` with the lock fd passed to npm, so the lock outlives an engine that dies mid-install; a launch takes the lock shared and an install takes it exclusively.
  host_terminal.py             Opens a native terminal on the engine's machine. The caller names an action id (`claude-install`, `claude-login`, `opencode-install`, `opencode-login`, `codex-install`, `codex-login`, `skills-update`, `opencode-update`, `claude-update`) or a chat/project, never a command or a path: commands are server-side constants or the functions that already produce the text Ciaobot shows (the two `*-update` ids take theirs from `provider_versions.current_checks()` and raise `NothingToUpdate`, a 409 `not_applicable`, when nothing is older than the tested version), and `resolve_folder` takes the folder from server state (the chat's code-mode worktree, else the project repo, else the workspace agent root). macOS opens Terminal (a self-deleting 0700 `.command` script for a command), Linux needs `DISPLAY`/`WAYLAND_DISPLAY` and picks the first known terminal. The terminal gets the engine's environment minus secrets and, in the bundled engine, minus the launcher's `SSL_CERT_FILE` and `PYTHONDONTWRITEBYTECODE` (`os_support/child_env.py`). Settings → Connect (`POST /api/settings/providers/{id}/connect`) launches `<id>-login` through it, behind `host_browser_request`.
  terminal_auth.py             Bounded terminal authorization grants (#1682, child of #1680): the browser terminal's credential, and only that — a foundation with no route, no shell and no session behind it. A grant is a 256-bit random bearer (minted by `issue` and handed to the caller exactly once, which puts it in a host-only, HttpOnly, SameSite=Strict `ciao_terminal` cookie) bound to the SHA-256 of the login cookie it was minted on, because the login cookie cannot identify a browser: two sign-ins inside one second can produce identical login cookies, and a grant that could not tell them apart would let one browser's logout revoke another's. The store retains digests and safe metadata only — the bearer's SHA-256 digest, the session digest, the grant id, the deadlines — so no record it keeps carries the bearer or the cookie; the raw bearer lives in `issue`'s local variable and its return value, and from there it is the caller's to keep. The grant id is a separate random 128-bit identifier, never the bearer. The lifetime is fixed at 15 minutes and never slides on verify, at most 32 grants are live at once, expired grants are reclaimed, and revocation is explicit: `revoke` ends the one grant a logout presents (not every grant sharing a login cookie) and `revoke_all` ends them all on a password change. A renewal revokes the grant it names only when that grant is bound to the same session, so a foreign bearer frees no slot and ends nothing. A credential that is not valid UTF-8 is a value, never an exception: an unusable session is refused and an unusable bearer or cookie verifies as nothing. There is no password in this module and no guessing budget: the signed-in session is the terminal's trust boundary, so the grant answers which browser may hold the socket and for how long, and the route that mints it asks the engine for nothing else — the unlock takes no body at all. The store authenticates nobody: a route must verify the session cookie itself, and it hands the store the session cookie the grant is bound to. In memory only: a grant is a short-lived capability, and a restart ends every one of them.
  chat_actions.py              The pure contract for a chat's transient needs-you actions: kinds, states, per-kind param validators, the dedupe key, payload, ids. The `command` kind's text contract lives here too (`normalize_command`: printable text only, line feeds and tabs allowed, CRLF normalized, ≤ 4096 UTF-8 bytes before and after normalization, ESC and every invisible or bidi character refused, so a bracketed paste cannot be closed early; a refusal names a static reason, never the text).
  terminal_replay.py           Bounded raw-byte replay for the browser terminal (#1705, child of #1680): the byte contract a reconnecting client replays from, and only that — no session, no process, no socket, no renderer, no persistence, and no runtime wiring yet. Terminal output is bytes, not text (a UTF-8 sequence or a VT sequence can be split across a read boundary), so the unit of position is the **absolute byte offset**: the total count of bytes the stream has produced, never rebased, and capped at `TERMINAL_REPLAY_MAX_OFFSET` (2**53-1) so every offset survives the trip to the browser as an exact JavaScript safe integer. Nothing is decoded, re-encoded or boundary-guessed here: a retained suffix may start halfway through a UTF-8 or escape sequence, and preserving the bytes exactly is the point, because repairing the screen is the renderer's job. Storage is one fixed `TERMINAL_REPLAY_BYTES` (256 KiB) `bytearray` and nothing else — no chunk registry, no full history, no output queue, no backlog — so the memory a terminal can hold is knowable in advance; the byte at offset `n` lives at `n % TERMINAL_REPLAY_BYTES`, which makes eviction a consequence of the write rather than a separate policy. The two losses are told apart, because they are different problems: a *stale initial* attach (an offset already evicted) is answered with a **truncated** snapshot of the retained suffix, so the caller resets the renderer and is honest about the gap, while a *live* read behind the oldest retained byte is refused as **`slow_consumer`** rather than silently clamped — a consumer that has lost bytes cannot be handed a stream with a hole in it, and giving it a second queue of output would be exactly the unbounded memory this module exists to prevent. One append and one read are each bounded at `TERMINAL_REPLAY_CHUNK_BYTES` (64 KiB), results are immutable copies that never alias the ring, and every refusal carries a closed reason (`invalid_offset`, `invalid_limit`, `chunk_too_large`, `sequence_exhausted`, `slow_consumer`) and a message that is the reason and nothing else. Tests: `tests/test_terminal_replay.py`.
  terminal_sessions.py         Bounded terminal session manager for the browser terminal (#1736, child of #1680): the one owner of the shells, and still no route, no WebSocket, no app wiring and no shell a user can reach — the transport-free half of the terminal, importable and testable on its own. It owns every process, every replay ring, every controller and every detached lifetime, and it keeps all four bounded. **Sessions**: at most `TERMINAL_MAX_SESSIONS` (4) are retained *in total*, exited shells included, because an exited shell still holds its output and its exit status for the UI; the slot is reserved *before* the shell is started, so a burst of creates cannot start a fifth process, and a spawn that fails, is cancelled or is overtaken by a shutdown closes the process it produced and releases the slot. **Output**: each session owns one `TerminalReplayBuffer` and nothing else — the ring's own 256 KiB window and absolute byte offsets are the contract, this module defines no second ring, no capacity and no chunk constant; a reader task polls the non-blocking process and appends each chunk once, so a client that falls behind the oldest retained byte is refused as `slow_consumer` rather than given a backlog, while an initial attach with a stale offset gets a `truncated` snapshot instead. **Controller identity**: a session has one optional *controller* — the random `connection_id` a route mints per attachment, never a grant fingerprint, never a cookie and never a browser, so two tabs of one browser are two controllers and the second one has to take over explicitly; a takeover replaces the controller and displaces the old attachment, and a stale detach, write or resize from the previous connection is refused rather than reaching the successor. **Lifetimes**: a detached session is pruned `TERMINAL_DETACHED_TTL_S` (900) after it became detached, and output never extends that deadline; an *attached* session is not pruned by this timer, because the unlock watchdog belongs to the route — but a shell that has *exited* has nothing left to control, so it releases its controller and ages out like any other detached record, keeping its final bytes and its exit code until it does. The expiry decision and the removal happen under one lock, so an attach that arrives in between cannot have its session closed under it. **Input**: bytes a terminal will not take stay pending, and the pending backlog is bounded, so nothing inside the engine grows without limit; one *flusher* task owns that backlog and is its only writer, so a terminal that will not take its input cannot stop its output, and a write that is cancelled can neither duplicate nor lose the bytes (they leave the queue before the write and exactly the refused remainder goes back). A write is *accepted*, not delivered: the caller never waits for a pipe that may block. **Lifetimes of a close**: a close is never abandoned — it runs in a manager-owned task that a cancelled caller cannot cancel, the process is marked closed only once the close has actually completed, and a shell is closed exactly once. `end()` closes the shell *before* it removes the record, so a cancelled end is retryable; `shutdown()` closes every shell before it clears the registry, so a cancelled shutdown still closes every shell. A close that still fails is logged, not retried: the platform adapter has already waited for its own threads. The process factory and the monotonic clock are injected (`spawn_terminal` and `time.monotonic` by default), which is how the tests reach every race without a pty and without waiting for a clock. Spawn, write, resize and close all run off the event loop, every task is observed and joined on end and shutdown, and nothing about the terminal's contents is written to a record, an error message or a log line — refusals are typed (`TerminalSessionError` with a closed reason). **Helper sessions**: `create(..., argv=...)` (engine-only; the routes never pass it) runs a fixed engine-built program instead of the shell; `helper_output`/`helper_input` read and type into a helper session without a controller and refuse a user shell (`invalid_target`). Tests: `tests/test_terminal_sessions.py`.
  terminal_scrape.py           Pure scraping of a helper terminal's output: strips OSC/CSI/ESC sequences, pulls https URLs (OSC 8 hyperlinks first, then plain text) and the last error line, all bounded to 64 KiB. Never logs or stores the bytes. Tests: `tests/test_terminal_scrape.py`.
  install_receipt.py           Installer-owned receipt (`~/.local/state/ciaobot/install-receipt.json`, 0600 in a 0700 directory, outside any tool environment or workspace) recording which engine version the terminal installer placed, which executables it owns, which service owns it, and the release it replaced. Written atomically via a temp file and validated first; read fail-safe, so a missing, corrupt, wrong-schema or unreadable file reads as "no receipt" instead of raising on the startup path. It only counts for a process whose interpreter lives in the receipt's environment (same resolved `bin` directory; the interpreter symlink itself is not followed, since every venv on one base Python would match), so a receipt left by an uninstall or a dev checkout sharing the machine cannot relabel this engine; `package_version.detect_install_mode()` reports `installer` from it, after bundled_app and editable. CLI: `python -m ciao.install_receipt write …`.
  package_version.py           Best-effort version probe: reads ciao.__version__ and queries the GitHub releases API for the latest published version. Powers GET /api/package/status. Also detects the app-bundled engine (`.app/Contents/Resources/engine/`): `running_from_app_bundle()`, `app_engine_root()`, `detect_install_mode()` = `bundled_app`. In the bundled engine `ensure_ciao_shim()` writes a `#!/bin/sh` shim that execs the engine's `bin/ciao` to `~/Library/Application Support/Ciaobot/engine/bin/ciao` (atomic, 0755, rewritten at start when stale); that directory, not the engine's `bin`, goes first on the child PATH so `python3` is not shadowed.
  package_smoke.py             Package smoke target: build web, install the project in a clean venv, and probe the installed app. CLI: `ciao package-smoke`.
  subprocess_step.py           Subprocess step runner for the dev deploy: a missing binary becomes exit 127 and a timeout 124, both as a failed CompletedProcess, so a deploy step reports as data instead of raising a 500.
  stock/                       Generic package-data assets for public installs: commands, skills, launchd template, workspace docs, and system schedules. No built-in subagents ship; memory-write guidance lives in `skills/ciao-capabilities/references/memory-operations/`. The unattended Workspace care run is split: `schedules.json` holds the purpose, ground rules, pass order and report format (under 8 KB), and each pass's procedure is a reference file in `skills/ciao-capabilities/references/memory-operations/references/` (`curation-pass-*.md`, `note-verify.md`, `curation-report-example.md`) that the run reads only for the passes `curation-begin` plans.
    skills/                    Packaged generic skills (ciao-capabilities and more). Installed into every workspace's `.claude/skills/` by `ciao sync-skills`; a same-named workspace skill overrides the packaged copy. Ciaobot support guidance is a reference under `ciao-capabilities`.
    public/                    Public extraction allowlist file (AGENTS.md), copied by public_release.py.
    workspace/                 Agent-readable docs copied into installed workspaces (`AGENTS.md`, `CIAO_CUSTOMIZATION.md`).
    schedules.json             Per-workspace system schedules (Workspace care).
    evals/scenarios.json       Synthetic behavioral-eval scenarios (recall, abstention, supersession, attended/unattended extraction, approval deferral, tool choice, isolation, injection). No real vault content; consumed by `ciao eval` via `ciao/behavioral_eval.py`.
    evals/tasks/               Agentic task-eval catalog: one `<task_id>/task.json` per task (prompt or delegate entry, setup steps, grading checks), plus `_fixture/`, the synthetic base vault every run copies in, and optional per-task `overlay/` trees. Consumed by `ciao/task_eval.py`; no real vault content.
  web/                         PWA web server (Python routes) + static assets (built by web/).
    app.py                     Starlette app factory: middleware, route table, SPA catch-all.
    auth.py                    Session-cookie auth middleware and serializer.
    request_limits.py          HTTP /api body boundary installed inside auth: route-matched finite upload/write caps (and the 32 KiB file-mention batch cap), bounded spooled replay, and JSON syntax/depth validation before dispatch.
    loop_watchdog.py           Outermost HTTP timing middleware (logs requests of 1 s or more, path without query) and a lifespan event-loop watchdog that logs stalls of 250 ms or more with the requests in flight (#2349). Warnings go to the root logger: stderr (`.runtime/ciao.stderr.log` under the LaunchAgent) and `server_debug.log` at debug level.
    security.py                Security response headers middleware.
    routes_api.py              REST route handlers. Catch-all for handlers without a domain home. The vault read handlers (`/api/vault/backlinks`, `/api/vault-markdown-paths`) do their whole file traversal in a bounded worker via `ciao/async_reads.run_read`, so a large-vault scan cannot stall WebSocket keepalives or unrelated responses. The chat-history handlers keep only their transport — query params, the pagination envelope, the part cache and the 404 — and hand the rendering to `transcript_service.py`. The category edit surface (`/api/memory/entity-types`, one handler for `GET`+`PATCH` as in `settings_routines`) is the same split: the route resolves `?workspace=` to the agent vault root, counts notes per `type:` off a worker-thread scan, maps 400/409/500, and hands the list to `entity_types.parse_payload`/`validate_entries`/`write_vault_file`; the domain rules (what a row means, what is persisted, what is refused) live in `entity_types.py`, and the Categories section of the regenerated `VOCABULARY.md` in `vault_index.py`.
    routes_onboarding.py       Signed-session browser transport for lifecycle progress and finish-gated completion. It accepts progress fields only, serializes mutations with the existing workspace registry lock, and delegates receipt operations to onboarding modules.
    document_conversion.py     Lazy firecrawl-anydoc conversion for chat attachments.
    routes_auth.py             Auth login/logout/check routes.
    routes_chat.py             Chat WebSocket + events routes.
    routes_chat_actions.py     POST /api/chats/{id}/actions/{aid}/start|dismiss|continue: browser-only action routes.
    routes_node.py             Package status, changelog and update routes (`/api/package/*`).
    routes_agent.py            `POST /agent/v1/{op}` for the agent CLI (bearer-authenticated by the agent registry, outside the PWA session cookie), `GET /api/agent/status` and `GET /api/mcp/usage` (session-gated agent-surface telemetry, no PWA consumer).
    routes_push.py             Web Push notification routes.
    routes_webhooks.py         Session-authenticated webhook trigger management (list/create/update/rotate/delete over the store at `<runtime>/webhooks.json`) plus the receipt-history reads (#1044): `GET /api/webhooks/{trigger_id}/receipts?workspace=` and `GET /api/webhooks/receipts?workspace=`, the operator's answer to "what has this trigger received and which chat did each event become" over the journal `routes_hooks.py`'s receiver writes. Workspace-scoped like the management routes — an unregistered `?workspace=` is a 400 and a foreign trigger is the same 404 as an unknown id — and rows are `WebhookReceipt.to_public_dict` alone, so no verifier, idempotency key or body digest can leave. The read is `WebhookReceiver.recent_receipts_for`/`recent_receipts`, bounded by `RECEIPTS_HISTORY_LIMIT` (50) and read backwards through `_iter_rows_newest_first` rather than folding the whole journal as a dedupe decision must. This module owns both `webhook_store` and `webhook_receiver`, so the ingress route and the history reads cannot drift onto two different journals. The ingress receiver itself is `routes_hooks.py`, not a bearer-authenticated surface here.
    routes_hooks.py            `POST /hooks/v1/{trigger_id}`, the webhook ingress receiver (#1010, child A3 of #974) — the one *machine* surface: authorized solely by one trigger's own bearer secret, never by the PWA session cookie, which is also why this route owns every control itself rather than inheriting one. `AuthMiddleware` protects `/api/*` and `/ws/*` only, so a `/hooks/` path gets no session check and no `_state_change_origin_allowed` check and falls straight through to the router. In order: a 405 for every method but POST — refused by its own second route for the same path, because the SPA catch-all below is a full match for every GET and a `methods=["POST"]` route would never get to refuse one — then the same origin predicate `/api/*` uses for a browser-shaped `Origin`/`Referer` (defence in depth — a browser cannot set `Authorization` without a preflight this origin never answers), `WebhookStore.authenticate` *before* the body is read (with `PWA_HOST=0.0.0.0` the route is LAN-reachable, and buffering first would fill memory before the 401), the `Idempotency-Key` header before the body is buffered, then the body twice over — declared `Content-Length` and again while reading, because a chunked request declares nothing. Missing/disabled/revoked/wrong secret are one 401; a store this code cannot read is a 500, because answering "no" to everything would turn a corrupt store into a sender that blames its own secret. 202 answers `{receipt_id, trigger_id, status}` — a durable receipt and no model output — with typed refusals mapped to 400/409/413/429/503 and a `Retry-After` on the 429. Dispatch (#1020) is *scheduled* here, never awaited: `schedule_webhook_dispatch` hands the accepted receipt to `webhook_dispatch.dispatch_receipt` as a background task kept in `_DISPATCH_TASKS`, so the response is a receipt rather than a launch result and a turn that runs for minutes never holds a sender's connection open. A missing `app.state.project_chat_manager` or a missing event loop is logged, not raised, and leaves the receipt `accepted` for the startup sweep.
    routes_host_terminal.py     GET/POST /api/host/terminal: whether this browser can open a terminal on the engine's machine, and the launch. Normal session path plus `host_browser_request` (a remote browser gets `not_on_host`: 200 on GET, 403 on POST); the POST body is exactly `{"action": id}` or `{"open": "folder", chat_id|project_id}`. Thin: platform behaviour, the allowlist and folder resolution live in `ciao/host_terminal.py`.
    routes_terminal.py          Browser-terminal unlock/status boundary: signed session even on loopback, exact scheme/hostname/effective-port origin policy (a write needs a bare `Origin`; a read may use a same-origin page `Referer`, whose path and query are its own and are permitted, while a fragment is refused; userinfo is refused by presence), effective HTTPS remotely, narrowly gated direct-loopback HTTP. No body at all on the unlock — the signed-in session is the terminal's trust boundary, so the route mints the grant for a signed-in, same-origin browser and refuses a request that carries one — plus a shared bounded grant store/clock, an HttpOnly Strict host-only grant cookie and explicit credential-free status fields. The route's own policy is stricter than the app's general origin rule, and the ordinary `AuthMiddleware` origin check can refuse a write before the handler is reached, so the two answer with different envelopes. No shell/session/WebSocket launch here.
    routes_terminal_sessions.py  The browser terminal's session surface (#1799, child of #1680): `GET`/`POST /api/terminal/sessions` and `DELETE /api/terminal/sessions/{session_id}`, the wire half of the one manager in `ciao/terminal_sessions.py`. The same door as the unlock, in the same order — the terminal policy, the signed session, a fresh grant — so a request that is not this engine's own page never has its body read, and a browser that has not unlocked never reaches a session (no grant is `403 locked`). The caller names a *target* and never a path or a command: exactly one of `chat_id` or `project_id`, and the folder comes from `ciao.host_terminal.resolve_folder` alone, which is also what decides whether the target exists (`ValueError` 400, `LookupError` 404, `FileNotFoundError` 409). The manager's typed refusals keep their own reasons and their own statuses (`not_found` 404, `capacity` 409, `invalid_target` 400, `closed` 409, `spawn_failed` 500, anything else 400). A record is `{session_id, folder, cols, rows, seq, attached, exited, exit_code}` — no output bytes and no connection id, so a bystander cannot impersonate the connection that holds a session — and every response is `no-store`. The app builds the one manager, starts it in the lifespan and closes it through the shutdown callbacks.
    routes_terminal_ws.py       The browser terminal's byte stream (#1800, child of #1680): `WS /ws/terminal/{session_id}`, the socket that carries a session to the browser. It owns no shell and no grant: the shells belong to `ciao/terminal_sessions.py` and the access policy to `ciao/web/routes_terminal.py`, and this module asks both for what it needs. **The door** is the HTTP half's door in the same order — the terminal policy (4403), the app's own `authorize_websocket` (4003/4001), a live grant (4401) — and every refusal is a close code, not a body; a session that is gone is 4404. All of it runs before the socket is accepted and before the manager is asked for anything, and the handshake carries no credential in its query string: the signed session and the `ciao_terminal` grant travel in cookies only. The grant is re-checked while the socket is open — a watchdog at most once a second, and again for every client message — so a revoked grant closes the socket with 4401 instead of leaving a shell open behind it. **The first frame** is due within five seconds and is exactly `{"type":"attach","offset":<int>,"takeover":<bool>}`; the offset is an absolute byte count, not an index, and a bool or a float is not one. Anything else is 4403. **The controller** is a random `connection_id` minted per attachment, never a grant or a cookie: a second connection needs `takeover:true` and is otherwise 4409, and a takeover displaces the old attachment with a 4409 of its own. **The stream** is bytes, addressed by absolute offset: `ready` (the snapshot, taken with the controller assignment), the replay frames, `replay_end`, then `output` frames `{start,end,data}` over `[start,end)` with strict base64 — and the client's cursor advances only over the bytes the server actually sent. A consumer that fell behind the 256 KiB ring is 4413 rather than a stream with a hole in it. **The client** sends `input` (strict base64, at most 64 KiB decoded), `resize` (the manager's own bounds) and `ping`; a frame the protocol does not describe is `{"type":"error","reason":"bad_frame"}` and a 1002 close, so frame spam keeps no state. **The end** detaches and never ends: the shell keeps running and the session stays recoverable for its detached lifetime, and only the explicit end route kills a shell. Tests: `tests/test_terminal_stream.py`.
    device_code_auth.py        The device_code needs-you card: a constant table of tools (gh, az) with fixed args, code regex and allowlisted verification URL; runs the tool as a helper terminal session for the chat, scrapes the one-time code, presses Enter for gh, done on exit 0, ends the helper in every terminal state. Tests: tests/test_device_code_auth.py.
    routes_welcome.py           GET /api/welcome, GET /api/welcome/password, POST /api/welcome/open-folder, POST /api/welcome/dismiss: the one-time welcome card, gated on `.runtime/welcome-pending`. Session path; the password read and the folder open also need `host_browser_request`; dismiss deletes the marker for every device.
    routes_service_login.py     GET/PATCH /api/service/login: the host engine's verified start-at-sign-in state and its one switch. The workspace is `app.state.config.workspace_root` and nothing else, the PATCH body must be exactly `{"enabled": bool}` and is rejected before any OS call otherwise, and the mutation is serialized by a module `asyncio.Lock` because the service re-inspects inside it. Thin: the refusal rules, the platform branches, and every OS call live in `ciao/service_login.py`.
    routes_tasks.py             `/api/tasks*` (list/get/create/patch/delete/complete/order/delegate/attempts/attempt-action), where `/order` repositions one card inside its own status column and a `placement` on a status-changing patch or complete is validated by the same helper, the session-authenticated task-board surface over the same workspace-scoped service that backs `ciao task …`: a request may only *name* a workspace, and the control plane resolves that name to its own vault, so no HTTP-supplied root ever reaches the store; the three state-changing task routes require the `expected_revision` the caller read and answer 409 on a stale one; `/complete` acts as `actor="user"` (this is the signed-in user's own session) while the agent CLI's `task_action` acts as `actor="agent"`: it completes only with the user's `reason` (stored as the resolution, logged as a `complete` decision, learned from), is refused `completion_requires_user` for `move`/status routes and refused a linked task, and nothing in these HTTP routes completes a task on an agent's behalf. The delegation routes (#1033, #1040) add `POST /delegate` (only `project_id` and the user's optional hand-over `instructions` beyond the workspace and the revision, because the prompt is built server-side from the record and the instructions are quoted after the description in their own fence rather than replacing it), `GET /attempts` (one task's history, live attempt first), `POST /attempt/{id}/{action}` for `stop`/`resume`/`retry`/`detach`, the last carrying the path's own `task_id` to the service, which answers a mismatch with 404 rather than acting on the attempt under somebody else's card. Transport only — request parsing, the workspace check, and the one typed-refusal-to-status table; every task rule and every file read lives in `ciao/task_board.py`, `ciao/task_attempts.py` and `ciao/control_plane.py`. Nothing here completes a task on an agent's behalf.
    routes_import.py            The import consent surface (#1029, C5 child of #975): `GET /api/import/sources?workspace=` returns the whole `import_discover.DiscoveryResult` — offered refs, excluded rows with their reasons, sources that could not be listed, per-source `truncated` — and `POST /api/import/preview` returns the confirmation payload for the *selected* refs (per-conversation counts, the source's own first date, the reader's omissions, the effective provider/model, an input-volume estimate, `batch_cap` and the destination). Session-cookie gated like every other `/api/*` route, with no public or loopback-only exception and no origin exception of its own; the POST takes the ordinary `_state_change_origin_allowed` path. Transport only: an unregistered or empty workspace is a 400 rather than the install root, because a listing answers "what can be imported into *this* workspace"; an unreadable Ciaobot registry is a 500 rather than a listing, because the scan would then be unable to exclude Ciaobot's own sessions. A selection names a source, never a location — the body is `{provider, source_id}` pairs and `import_discover` rebuilds every path from the workspace's configured root, so there is no parameter through which a caller could hand this module a path. No transcript text is returned: the rows carry counts, dates and omission kinds, so the selection screen cannot leak the conversation it is asking about. No model call, no proposal write — the batch store is C6. Since #1038 (C7) this module also owns `POST /api/import/batches/{batch_id}/run`, which is the only route here that extracts anything: it moves the batch to `running`, schedules `ciao.import_run.run_import_batch` on the event loop and answers 202 immediately, so **no turn runs inside the request**; a body naming a model or provider is ignored (the configuration decides: the per-provider insights model the runner resolves for the source's own provider, which is not necessarily the model `preview_selected` reported), a batch already `running` is answered 200 with its current state rather than starting a second run, and a settled batch is a 409. Both handlers run in a worker thread: the OpenCode listing shells out under a 60s timeout and neither belongs on the event loop that serves the WebSockets. Since #1041 (C8) `GET /api/import/batches` also answers `retention_days`, read from `import_store.IMPORT_SNAPSHOT_RETENTION_DAYS` rather than spelled out: it is the same number the once-per-boot sweep prunes on, and the import panel states it as the retention it enforces instead of carrying a second copy in the PWA.
    routes_helpers.py          Shared route helpers (api_error envelope, workspace path resolution, git sync).
    proposal_service.py        Proposal-queue domain logic behind the proposal routes: scanning the per-workspace `Workspace/Memory-Proposals.md` queue and `Workspace/Skill-Proposals/` folder, the locked single/batch queue rewrites, re-home signalling and moves, and the promotions an accept performs (region write, doc fold, people note, learnings append). routes_api.py imports it as a module and keeps request parsing, authorization, and response mapping. What the resolved row then *reports* and where its decision is *recorded* is not here: that is `ciao/proposal_actions.py`, one level up, because the CLI resolves rows too and must not import the web package to do it. What a promotion *did* is `AcceptOutcome`, a frozen dataclass owned here: every accept helper answers with one instead of a hand-built `dict[str, Any]`, and `as_dict()` emits exactly the keys that branch produced — absent, never null, since `build_accept_result` reads an outcome with no `ok` at all as "nothing was written here", which is a success. The routes read its fields; only the hand-off to `proposal_actions` goes through `as_dict()`, so `proposal_actions` stays a plain `Mapping` consumer and never has to import `ciao.web`.
    update_task_launch.py       The launch half of the "After this update" pipeline (issue #761): `ciao/update_task_catalog.py` defines the tasks, `ciao/update_tasks.py` (#756) remembers their per-scope state and answers whether they apply, and this turns one eligible task into one chat — server-owned, and exactly once. **The prompt is server-owned**: it is read from the packaged resource by `read_prompt`, and no parameter accepts prompt text from a caller; what travels instead is `prompt_digest = sha256(prompt)[:16]`, so a later reader can tell whether the instructions changed without shipping them twice. **Start is idempotent per `(task id, revision)`**: the read of the record and the write that replaces it are one `update_tasks._record_lock` section (the same one the rest of #756 uses), so a double click, two tabs, a retry after a dropped response and a restart all get the SAME chat back — `resumed: true`, nothing created, nothing dispatched. A record naming a chat that is gone is the recoverable case: a fresh chat is created and the record re-stamped to it. Because records are keyed `"<id>@<revision>"`, a revised task cannot resume, or even see, the previous revision's chat, so an engine update can never substitute new instructions into a running chat. The record is written BEFORE the turn is dispatched, so a failed dispatch cannot orphan the chat: `UpdateTaskLaunchError` carries the id, the attempt is marked `failed`, and the next start sends the prompt into that same chat. The pre-dispatch write is `failed` and the flip to `in_progress` happens only once `start_stream` has returned, so a lifecycle in the file means what it says and a crash in that window is retried rather than reported as a resume that never ran; a write that *fails* in the same window is logged, not raised, because `UpdateTaskStateError` is a `ValueError` and a 409 would answer a running turn with no `chat_id` and invite the retry that dispatches it twice. A start on a `dismissed` record is a reopen: same chat, record written `in_progress`, nothing re-sent — which is only sound because `dismiss_task` drops the chat of a record that is `failed`, the one lifecycle that means the prompt never reached it, so a dismissal can never hand a later start an empty chat to call a running one. The chat is hosted in the workspace's own `General` project — the one `HousekeepingStrip.vue` finds by its `is_auto` flag, created if the install has none — and carries an `update_task` helper (`task_id`, `revision`, `scope`, `prompt_digest`) normalised fail-closed in `chat_service._normalize_chat_helper`, which is what makes the chat findable again after a restart. No model call, no detector, no completion check, no `eval`, no shell and no remote fetch: a start is a decision the operator has already made. `reopen_task` is a thin wrapper over `update_tasks.reopen_task` and `dismiss_task` is the same write plus the failed-chat rule, so a card and a direct call cannot produce two records for one decision; that rule stays on the launch side because `update_tasks` never opens a chat. A `dismissed` or reopened-`offered` record with a live chat is un-declined in place (`in_progress`, same chat, nothing re-sent) so `dismiss → start` and `dismiss → reopen → start` agree, and a `completed` record is refused outright with a `ValueError` before the chat is looked at — by a start *and* by a dismissal, so no stale tab pressing either one, and no archived or deleted chat, can turn a finished task back into a running one. Routes: `GET /api/update-tasks?workspace=`, and `POST /api/update-tasks/{task_id}/{start,dismiss,reopen}`; each state-changing one carries the fresh task rows, dropped rather than raised if the listing fails. Each row reports **two clocks** and they are not merged: `applicability_checked_at` is when that row's answer was computed (`update_tasks.ApplicabilityResult.checked_at`, a wall-clock stamp taken where the detector ran) and `updated_at` is when the record was written. A dismissed task therefore has a decision time and a possibly-stale check time, and a client that showed one under the other's name would be claiming a re-check that never happened. Inside `APPLICABILITY_TTL_S` a row keeps the stamp of the call that computed the answer, so a repeated read does not re-date it; the state file is re-read every call regardless.
    agent_assets.py            Agent-facing instruction/subagent/command asset endpoints and OS audit.
    commands.py                Slash-command listing and rate-limit endpoints.
    connection_tracker.py      Tracks live WebSocket connections; `chat_client_count` answers how many browsers have a given chat open.
    chat_broker.py             Chat WebSocket broker, file-touch tagging, snapshot scheduling.
    project_chats.py           ProjectChatManager: project/chat lifecycle coordinator. Its streaming, scheduled-dispatch, and archive-postprocess lifecycles are being extracted as collaborators one at a time (#499); the manager keeps admission, persistence, archive APIs, and announcement policy rather than the transient state those lifecycles own. Admission stages retry reset, eligible memory helper/source transitions, cards and turn/send-ID metadata synchronously and commits them in one `_save` before registering the stream or changing retry/drain tasks. A precommit failure restores only staged fields in place, retaining ChatInfo/container identities and the complete send-ID history, without a compensating save. The existing exclusive state lock still encloses latest-read/field-level-merge/replacement. Successful sibling-temp replacement is the commit point: the independent local diff baseline advances immediately, and escaping audit/cleanup/unlock failures become `_RegistryCommittedError(OSError)` rather than undoing a committed admission. This is atomic replacement, not a stronger power-loss or task-allocation guarantee.
    chat_service.py            Chat-workflow domain logic behind ProjectChatManager: provider-error retry classification, fork/handover transcript trimming, chat-title derivation, project-doc frontmatter description writes, project-file upload policy, and scheduled-run grading (`ScheduleRunOutcome`). project_chats.py and routes_api.py import it as a module; the manager keeps the state file, the live streams, and the background tasks.
    chat_streaming.py          The chat drive-loop collaborator, extracted from ProjectChatManager (#499). `ChatStreaming` owns the admitted stream's multi-turn drive loop, one-pass `stream_chat`/crash-journal/provider aggregation, the between-turns provider drain, per-turn latency clocks, and drain/task registries. A drive waits on every predecessor before it enters the provider: the registered between-turns drain (joined, never popped, so a second waiter shares the same unwind) and a background Stop's shared handoff (the drain unwind plus the OpenCode abort). A Stop during that wait withdraws the prompt to the waiting chip, and a drain cancelled by a withdrawn Stop is replaced by a chained drain that starts after the old one ends. A drain whose events arrive after it was cancelled or superseded by a foreground admission touches no chat state and publishes no `chat_streaming_done`. `ProjectChatManager.start_stream` remains the admission/setup boundary: it decides whether a stream may start, registers the `ChatStream`, writes the user echo and turn metadata, and publishes `chat_streaming_started`; the manager coordinates request/capability construction, persistence decisions, and the result-announcement/parking policy that the drive and drain call back into. All dependencies cross the explicit `ChatStreamingHost` Protocol with concrete return types; there is no new `Any`-based collaborator contract. The manager keeps delegating methods under the old names (`stream_chat`, `_drive_stream`, the drain methods, and `_wait_for_drain_result`) and exposes `_turn_perf_started`, `_between_turn_drains`, and `_last_drain_result` as views onto the collaborator's state, so existing patch points and private-state fixtures still exercise the moved code; general detached-task retention remains a manager concern. `tests/test_chat_streaming_boundary.py` drives the provider pass and WebSocket lifecycle against a stub host and pins those ownership views; the existing chat, retry, capability-preflight, Stop, and subagent suites pin the manager-facing behavior.
                              Admission registers and buffers the echo only after persistence, then `start_drive` installs the drive task and its identity-safe cleanup before synchronous attended-turn observer notification; the event loop cannot advance the provider first. Observer/publication failures are isolated after ownership, and committed-save diagnostics cannot report a rejected send. Unrelated sends preserve pending retry ownership. A precommit rejection leaves any existing background drain untouched. Turn-end persistence failures retain the existing finish/teardown guarantee. Memory-pass admission uses mutation-only `prepare_turn_start`: both an attention continuation and `pump`'s private queued dispatch stage helper/source running state in that same single save. Prompt construction precedes dispatch. A rejected queued pass stays queued with no compensating attention write or recursive pump. The coordinator retains at most one 30-second `call_later` recovery wakeup per workspace after admission/prompt failure or when restart draining defers queued work; repeated deferrals do not postpone an existing wakeup. Timer callbacks delegate all eligibility/defer decisions to `pump`, which rechecks current FIFO and archived/running state. Success, an empty queue or another running pass cancels the wakeup; if that running pass finishes during draining, its completion pump creates a new wakeup so cancellation of the drain cannot strand queued work. `begin_shutdown` closes the coordinator and cancels all wakeups without saving, leaving startup resume to recover the persisted queue. Committed admissions never schedule recovery. Non-admission memory finish/enqueue/resume retain their saving setters.
    chat_actions_base.py       Needs-you handler base: ActionContext (pcm, chat, provider, is_loopback, app_state, terminals, agent_root) and the ActionHandler base class every kind subclasses (start, poll, cancel, callback, wake_prompt; start_states, poll_states).
    chat_actions_runtime.py    Needs-you action runtime: the handler registry (one handler per kind: command, restart, permission_mode, system_setting, device_code), the per-action poll loop and lifecycle cancel behind ChatActionsRuntime.
    schedule_dispatch.py       The scheduled-chat lifecycle collaborator, extracted from ProjectChatManager (#499). `ScheduleDispatcher` owns target-chat preparation (`prepare_schedule_chat`), the unattended stream and run-outcome grading in `dispatch_schedule`, the schedule attention classifier (`_schedule_run_needs_user`). `ProjectChatManager` remains the coordinator and keeps thin delegates under the old method names, so existing tests and integrations that patch `start_stream`, `prepare_schedule_chat`, `_schedule_run_needs_user`, `archive_chat`, or `run_archive_postprocess` still exercise the moved code. All host dependencies cross the explicit `ScheduleDispatchHost` Protocol with concrete return types; schedule storage is typed as `ScheduleStore`, and no new `Any`-based collaborator contract is introduced. The archive write API remains manager-owned; post-archive lifecycle work is in `archive_pipeline.py`. `tests/test_schedule_dispatch_boundary.py` drives the collaborator against a typed stub host and pins outcome classification, manager delegation, and patch seams.
    archive_pipeline.py        What happens after an archive is written (#499). `ArchivePipeline` queues the memory pass for the archived chat (unless no focus was given and either the chat belongs to a system schedule such as Workspace care, which is the memory machinery itself, or every user turn in the archive is unattended), indexes the archive for search, writes the archive capture record for an archive that enqueues a pass (best-effort: a failed write is logged and never fails the archive), publishes the chat's `postprocess` record (the memory pass's own step), and deletes a deleted chat's archived transcript directory; `ProjectChatManager` keeps the archive write API and registry persistence and delegates to it through the typed `ArchivePipelineHost` Protocol.
    memory_pass.py             The end-of-conversation memory pass as a normal chat (#602, behind the module-level `MEMORY_PASS_CHATS` constant, which is `True`). `MemoryPassCoordinator` owns the per-workspace `Memory` system project (`ensure_project`, id derived from the never-written `__memory__` vault folder so discovery cannot claim it and the name is free), the one-running-per-workspace FIFO queue (`enqueue`, `pump`), the turn-end settle (`on_turn_finished`: clean -> `done` + auto-archive, otherwise `attention` left open for the owner), and restart recovery (`resume`). Queue and running state live on each memory chat's `ChatInfo.helper` (`kind == "memory_pass"`, normalised fail-closed in `chat_service._normalize_chat_helper`) so a restart can see what was in flight. `is_memory_pass_chat` is the recursion guard: neither a pass chat nor anything in a `kind == "memory"` project is ever enqueued or given one-shot insights. `archive_pipeline.run_archive_postprocess` is the single hook, and `ProjectChatManager` keeps the thin seams (`enqueue_memory_pass`, `_memory_pass_turn_finished`, `resume_memory_passes`) plus the notification suppression: results, questions and permissions are all silent for a pass at the external transport boundary — the push callbacks are never invoked — while the pass's state, pending input and internal `chat_result_ready` lifecycle event stay intact. Because the project kind is that fallback, the engine keeps the Memory project true to its purpose: it holds the engine's own passes and nothing else — `create_chat` refuses an ordinary chat there (`MemoryProjectRefused`), `update_chat` refuses to move one in, a fork or continuation of a pass is placed in the workspace's General project instead, the create route refuses a client-supplied `memory_pass` helper, and the agent surface reports the refusal as `invalid_project`. A routine is a target like any other, so the scheduler refuses the same two: `prepare_schedule_chat` raises `MemoryProjectRefused` for a target the app will not run in — the caller records it, because a run that never happened is not a run — and the scheduler's tick catches it, marks the entry failed and carries on with the other due schedules, while the interval path disables the entry with the reason. The dispatch checks the target before a routine's command runs, and the schedule routes refuse such a target with a 400 that names the reason. `tests/test_memory_pass.py` covers the helper contract, the system project, the archive hook both ways, dedupe, queue order, clean/unclean settle, restart resume, the attended turn shape and the skill-review section. A declared pass (`helper.kind == "memory_pass"`) is deliberately not a chat in any PWA list: `web/src/stores/projects.ts` filters it out of `activeChatsAll` and `projectChats`, so it never reaches the Home tiers, the sidebar, the unread badge or a schedule target. It reaches the surface through `web/src/lib/memoryInsights.ts`, which joins the in-flight archive and the pass onto ONE Home row keyed on the archived conversation (a pass arriving while the archive request is still in flight joins the existing row rather than adding one), and through the archived source's own `Open memory pass` button. A pass whose helper failed closed in a Memory project has no insight row, so it stays in those ordinary lists as the way back to its pending question; every notification predicate also receives the project and keeps it silent. `chatIsAttentionItem` is the one rule every counter asks, and it is false for a pass even when blocked; Home's memory-insight row carries the blocked state and its question, and no notification surface (toasts, inbox, bell or OS badge, menu bar) includes a pass. The pass also reviews skills, and that is one extra paragraph on the prompt rather than a second chat: `SKILL_REVIEW_PROMPT` is appended only when the archived transcript names a skill `skills_inventory.eligible_owned_skills` resolves for that workspace, so the candidates are the backend's intersection of what the conversation used and what the workspace owns — never the catalog, and never a stock/mirror/shared copy, which are excluded by the resolver rather than by a flag the model supplies. The pass files a supported finding with `ciao skill-proposal-add` and never edits a skill; no proposal is a valid result. **A second, separate section** (`LESSON_ROUTING_PROMPT`, #728-D) covers the lessons in `Workspace/Learnings.md`, which is also where a `/remember` of a lesson lands. A lesson applies to a skill whether or not this conversation happened to load that skill, and the correction gate made that finding unrepresentable: there was no way to file it without claiming the transcript demonstrated something it did not. So the lesson path is its own candidate set (the workspace's own inventory, gated on `upstream_drafts.learnings_present` so a workspace with no lessons is never handed a catalog to walk), it records the link through the structured `origins` field rather than a `sources`/turn entry, and it says in the same breath that a catalog match is a candidate and not proof. The two destinations that are not an edit to an owned source are named there as `[review]` drafts through `ciao skill-draft-add`: an upstream issue for a packaged/mirrored/shared skill, and a new skill for a workflow no skill covers. The section renders on its own when the workspace owns no skills at all, because a workspace that owns nothing still has lessons with somewhere to go — it gets the two draft paths and no edit path. A workspace with no lessons and no used skill gets the memory prompt unchanged and spends no turns on any catalog. A pass may carry a focus (#1069): `approved_task`, queued when the user approves a delegated task, adds a section asking the pass to extract the procedure as skill proposals or new-skill drafts; it is deduplicated per chat and focus.
    restart_resume.py          Resume after restart (#2128): the process token a turn stamps on ChatInfo.turn_owner, the "Continue after restart" prompt and the skip rules; ProjectChatManager.resume_interrupted_turns runs them at boot.
    transcript_service.py      The chat transcript's read path: stored provider messages -> the rows the PWA renders. Ownership boundary: `routes_api.py` owns the HTTP surface of a chat, this module owns everything between what a provider stored and what the transcript shows. That is the per-message rules (which blocks an assistant message contributes, which tool calls earn a file card and which lose it when the call failed, which user turns are the CLI talking to itself rather than the human, which injected context is stripped back off a prompt), the per-session renderers (the Claude session JSONL, an ACP session journal), the assembly that stitches a chat's sessions, handover messages and durable-transcript metadata into one chronological list (`_assemble_chat_messages`), and the last shaping step before the wire (`_prune_rows_for_wire`). It imports no Starlette and takes no `Request`: callers pass the manager, the config and the chat. `routes_api.py` imports it as a module, not by name, so a test that patches one helper has the handlers see the patch. `tests/test_transcript_service_contract.py` pins the route table, the no-transport rule and the golden row payloads. A hidden continuation (`ciao chat reload`, or the legacy code-mode one in older transcripts) is still filtered before it renders, but first the reader annotates the existing preceding answer row `phase: "final_answer"` (`_mark_preceding_answer`); it never inserts a row, notice or user-turn index, so absolute wire indices and pagination stay stable. Archive/sessionless readers and live streaming are not changed. What counts as a CLI-synthesized user record is not decided here: that table is `ciao/cli_envelopes.py`. The renderer asks it whether a record is an envelope *before* asking for a task-notification summary, so a record that opens with a notification and carries anything after the closing tag still becomes a status line, while a human message quoting an envelope keeps its prose. A first notification that is a provider-native shell (`cli_envelopes.native_task_run`: Bash `run_in_background`, Monitor) becomes a `system` row with `origin: "background_run"` whose text is `background_notice.format_finished_runs`, so the PWA draws the background-run card; subagent completions keep the status line.
    file_snapshots.py          SnapshotStore: append-only file snapshots behind the file viewer.
    artifact_bridge.py         Comment bridge injected into /api/workspace-html responses: a thin in-frame agent that reports selections, forwarded keystrokes and Alt+Clicks over postMessage and paints comment highlights (CSS Custom Highlight API); the page owns the Comment button and composer.
    push.py                    PushManager: Web Push publishing, and cross-device read-clear controls.
  # Route handlers live in ciao/web/routes_<domain>.py. routes_api.py is the catch-all for
  # handlers without a domain home; new domains get their own routes_*.py rather than growing
  # routes_api.py. Domain logic a handler or a manager needs belongs in a service module beside it
  # (proposal_service.py, chat_service.py, transcript_service.py), not inline in the handler or the
  # manager. Logic TWO surfaces share — a route and the CLI — goes one level up into ciao/
  # (proposal_actions.py) instead, so the CLI never has to import ciao.web to reach it.
  # mcp_server.py owns the Ciao agent control plane and telemetry; routes_agent.py serves the agent CLI routes and `GET /api/mcp/usage`.
  macos_service.py             JSON launchd/service, engine-update, migration, and rollback surface behind `ciao service`. The reload path (`load_agent`) is unchanged. `LAUNCHCTL` pins `/bin/launchctl` by absolute path for the probes `mac_takeover` runs; the legacy load/unload path still invokes `launchctl` by name.
  mac_takeover.py              Read-only classification and owned takeover of a 1.x launchd install (#3129): `plan()` never writes; `apply`/`finish`/`rollback` own every journalled mutation under `state_dir()/takeover/<id>/`.
  server_host.py               The fail-closed macOS `Ciaobot Server.app` host contract (#1023, child B1 of #1008), in three deliberately separate layers. **Syntax** (`parse_service_command`): pure, cross-platform, `PurePosixPath`-parsed argv for the hosted `[Ciaobot Server, serve, --python, abs]`, the hosted move `[Ciaobot Server, run-move, --python, abs, --operation, id, --state-dir, abs]` and the direct `[python, [-I], -m, ciao.cli, run|supervise]` / `[ciao, run|supervise]` shapes — it names the host, it never proves it. `host_service_argv` and `host_move_argv` render the two hosted shapes and `host_interpreter` exposes the interpreter rule the host's own parser applies, so a caller can answer "can this host run this child?" before it renders anything; all three are syntax only, and the caller must prove ownership first. **Identity** (`inspect_host_bundle`, macOS only, injected runner): plist identity, exactly the two universal slices, per-arch CDHashes and a digest of exactly the policy's sealed files, through the read-only `/usr/bin/codesign` probes only, bounded and shell-free — a well-formed host, not ownership. The policy is explicit and finite (#2329): the production revision-4 host must pass a strict ad-hoc `codesign` check over its four sealed files, and the opt-in revision-5 host must satisfy the external Developer ID requirement for an expected team (whose format is validated before any requirement text exists), the builder's canonical designated requirement on every slice, the hardened-runtime flag with a secure timestamp, and exactly one `com.apple.security.automation.apple-events` entitlement, over its four files plus the `Contents/CodeResources` notarization ticket. A completed probe that disproves that policy is the stable `signature_rejected`, distinct from `inspection_failed`, and a failed signed check never falls back to ad-hoc acceptance. **Ownership** (`verify_owned_host`): an existing owner-only record outside the bundle, matched exactly on path/identity/revision/protocol/hashes/file-set (plus the signing identity for schema 2), is the only proof; a missing record is a refusal, never a direct-engine fallback. `read_host_ownership` is strict (each policy's own schema, revision and sealed file set; duplicate keys, traversal, owner/mode) and writes nothing, keeping schema-1 records byte-shape-compatible. Constants (`HOST_REVISION` 4, `HOST_PROTOCOL` 2, `MOVE_HOST_PROTOCOL` 2, `STOP_GRACE_SECONDS` 35, `EXIT_TIMEOUT_SECONDS` 45) tie the plist to the host; B2 owns the consumers; the installer (child E) owns the record write.
  linux_service.py             Side-effect-free systemd unit renderer and installer (`ciao linux-service` renders a unit; `ciao service install` plans and runs the privileged install through one `sudo sh -c` script, refusing to clobber a unit that runs another interpreter, and refusing one that records a different workspace or user than the unit it would write; a dangling link at the unit path still counts as existing, and a unit this account cannot stat is refused instead of overwritten). Linux setup initializes workspace data independently of service installation; the unit runs the virtualenv interpreter under a dedicated account.
  service_login.py             The one platform seam for the engine's start-at-sign-in state (#913), behind `GET`/`PATCH /api/service/login`. It answers two questions — will the service start at the next sign-in, and may that be changed from here — and answers them from the machine, never from what Ciaobot would have installed. Three rules hold on every branch. **Ownership first:** the status speaks only for a definition that names this engine's own workspace, so the macOS read takes the definition's actual `WorkingDirectory`/`EnvironmentVariables.CIAO_WORKSPACE` rather than the `CIAO_WORKSPACE` environment fallback `macos_service.discover_runtime` would reach for, and a definition for another workspace is reported and never repointed. **Unknown is an answer:** a malformed plist or a `launchctl print-disabled` listing that does not parse or contradicts itself reports `installed`/`enabled` as `None` with the reason, because a failed query is not evidence that no service exists. **A change is the enabled bit and nothing else:** macOS reuses `macos_service.set_login_enabled` (launchd enable/disable) and re-reads the machine afterwards, so a change is only a success when the re-read agrees — `LoginUnavailable` (503) instead of a claim. Nothing here starts, stops, restarts, bootstraps or registers a service, and every OS call is a fixed argv, captured, without a shell, and bounded. Linux and any platform with no backend answer `supported: false` and touch nothing. The sign-in page reads the listing through the lenient `_parse_disabled_listing`.
  service_backend.py          The one platform-neutral seam for per-user service operations (where definitions go, and how an agent is loaded, booted out, or handed off). macOS and Linux both write launchd-format plists and never run launchctl on Linux; any other platform raises UnsupportedPlatformError until a backend for it exists.
  update_host.py              The platform seam for the engine update transaction: one `UpdateHost` protocol, one implementation per platform, and `current_update_host()` picking between them the way `service_backend.current_backend()` does. Each method is one answer `engine_update` cannot give itself — the port the engine answers on, how it is stopped and started, the program the loaded service runs, how the one-shot updater job and the durable recovery job are written and started, the interpreter a recovery runs from, what a staged environment looks like and how the swap installs and re-points it, the well-known `uv`, and the signals that end a drain. `MacUpdateHost` wraps launchd, LaunchAgents and the POSIX env layout exactly as `engine_update` did before the seam existed, and anything else raises UnsupportedPlatformError until a host for it exists. `default_update_host()` is what the transaction uses when a caller names none: the macOS host (with the caller's `launchctl` and `uid`). The state machine, the drain, the rollback ordering and the record writes stay in `engine_update` and are the same on every platform, which is the point: only the mechanics move.

web/                           Vue 3 PWA frontend.
  src/App.vue                  Root component.
  src/main.ts                  App bootstrap.
  src/router.ts                Vue Router config.
  src/components/              UI components (chat, projects, settings, etc.). Two of them
                               are the "After this update" surface and the rule between
                               them is the point: `HousekeepingStrip.vue` renders the
                               operator-action tiles and the update-task group as two
                               separate `<section>`s, never one list. The tiles are a
                               *view of the machine* — a blocking precondition among them
                               is unmissable and non-dismissible, and it has to be read
                               first — while an update task is a *decision* that persists
                               until somebody reverses it and can be reopened from
                               Settings. Merged into one list, an optional "start this
                               task" card could sort above an unmissable blocker, and one
                               "hide it" button would have to mean two different things.
                               Each lifecycle gets the action it alone can use: `offered`
                               Start in chat, `in_progress` Resume chat, `waiting_review`
                               Review proposals (the queue that has per-row accept/dismiss
                               and a destination picker, rather than re-deciding them in
                               prose) plus Resume, `failed` Try again, and `unknown`
                               applicability a quiet Check again and *no* start button at
                               all — nobody can say the work applies, and offering the
                               button would invite running instructions for a condition
                               this install has not established exists. The gate is the
                               lifecycle and not the detector: a *live* attempt
                               (`in_progress`/`waiting_review`/`failed`) always keeps
                               Resume or Try again, because `not_applicable` is the
                               normal state once the chat has done the work but
                               `record_completion` has not confirmed it, and gating on
                               applicability there would strand an open chat behind a
                               bare "Open its chat" link. A `not_applicable` offer with
                               no attempt is not a card at all — every install ships
                               the whole catalog, so drawing those would put a
                               permanent "nothing to do" on the Home of installs that
                               need none of it, and an install with no work has to have
                               an empty Home. The group outlives its last card only long
                               enough to announce what was pressed (a fixed 10s, ended
                               early by a workspace switch, since the announcement
                               describes a press in the workspace being left) and to
                               take the focus that the removed button dropped.
                               **Hiding is not
                               completing**: it records "not this one" for this
                               `(task, revision)` in this scope, the chat stays open and is
                               not marked done, the confirmation says both, and Settings →
                               Update task history can reopen it. Every transition surfaces
                               a refusal in place (a 409 is mapped to plain recovery
                               guidance) rather than clearing the card, because a cleared
                               card reads as "handled". A transition that lost a
                               workspace race reports its outcome but never adopts the
                               reply's rows or re-lists, the same guard
                               `refreshUpdateTasks` applies to its own answer. The group
                               is fetched on the same
                               cadence as the strip but as a *separate* request, so
                               `/api/housekeeping` keeps being nothing but a cheap
                               detector pass, and a failed list is silent rather than a red
                               line on Home — Settings is where a failed check is reported.
                               **Import conversations** (#1041, C8) is one flow in two
                               components plus one store, and the split is by lifetime:
                               `ImportSources.vue` owns what does not survive the page (the
                               opt-in scan, the client-side selection, and the consent screen
                               that files a batch), `ImportRuns.vue` owns what does
                               (`web/src/stores/import.ts`: the filed batches, their progress,
                               the cancel, the per-fact provenance and the retention the sweep
                               enforces), and both read
                               `web/src/components/importSources.css` so a row, a disclosure
                               and a quiet button measure the same on either side of the
                               "file this batch" line. The store mirrors `webhooks`'
                               workspace and sequence guards — a batch id and its provenance
                               name one workspace's private store, so a late answer is dropped
                               rather than drawn under a workspace that has never heard of it
                               — and it holds the retention window the list route reports
                               rather than a copy of the number. Both halves keep the five
                               list states apart (first load, first-load failure, stale
                               refresh, empty, filtered-empty), because a failed read must
                               never claim an import is gone and "nothing matches the filter"
                               is not "nothing was ever filed". The page is the only entry:
                               the first-run welcome names it and Memory reaches it at any
                               time, and nothing here describes a flow that is not
                               implemented.
  src/components/settings/     The Settings panels (`SettingsView.vue` hosts them on the
                               route's tab, each owning its own fetch). `SettingsUpdateTasks.vue`
                               is the update-task history: grouped by scope (install first),
                               listing the completed/dismissed/failed and genuinely
                               uncertain rows — the ones still on offer or in flight are Home's
                               job, and showing them twice would put one live card in two
                               places with two button sets. Read-mostly: the only write is a
                               Reopen, which re-evaluates applicability rather than replaying
                               old instructions. It shows `Checked` (when a detector last
                               produced this row's answer) beside `Decided` (when the record
                               was written) as two separate times, because a dismissal is a
                               decision and not a re-check, and a history that merged the two
                               could not tell an unexamined condition from a settled one.
                               `NotificationBell.vue` is the inbox's bell (sidebar top row, collapsed rail, and the pane header on phones) and its popover or phone sheet; `NotificationInboxList.vue` is the inbox body inside it. It renders `stores/notificationInbox.ts` and calls no source mutation; a row whose chat, task or automation has gone is re-read and shows *That item is no longer available* rather than navigating. `HomeNoticeWindow.vue` and `composables/useHomeNoticeWindows.ts` are presentation only: a `/?notice=<key>` reveal reopens, scrolls to and focuses that one window, and a closed window stays closed for the page without touching any inbox row.
  src/stores/                  Pinia stores, plus store modules: a `create*` factory that owns one
                               slice of a store's state and is instantiated inside that store's setup
                               (chatAnnotations.ts, owned by projects.ts). A slice with its own
                               localStorage keys and no dependency on the rest of the store belongs in
                               a module; splitting it into a second Pinia store instead would change
                               what consumers see.
                               `notificationInbox.ts` is the inbox's adapter. It owns no data: it reads `projects`,
                               `taskSignals`, `housekeeping` and `tasks` (not `proposals` or `vaultReview`: memory
                               suggestions and notes to revisit are not inbox content), owns the
                               init/dispose lifecycle that `ChatLayout` drives, and no store imports it, so the
                               dependency runs one way.
  src/lib/                     Helpers (API client, formatters, etc.). Pure, and free of Vue imports:
                               logic a store can hand over whole (chatHistory.ts, chatQuestions.ts,
                               chatWs.ts) goes here so it is testable without Pinia or a mount.
                               The memory pass's whole PWA surface starts here: `memoryPass.ts` is the
                               only reader of the `memory_pass` helper kind (`isMemoryPassChat`,
                               `memoryPassState`, `memoryPassSource`, `memoryPassChatId`), and
                               `memoryInsights.ts` derives Home's one row per archived conversation from
                               the archive pipeline plus that pass — every phase, its wording and its
                               flags — so the lane rows and the lane status sentence cannot disagree.
                               The embedded terminal's screen starts here too: `terminalRenderer.ts`
                               owns one physical xterm bound to one session — the session id is fixed
                               at construction, xterm's options object never leaves the module, input
                               is off until `setInputEnabled(true)`, a write is acknowledged only from
                               xterm's own write callback with the absolute end offset of the bytes
                               just parsed, a write still in flight when the screen is disposed is
                               rejected rather than resolved, the theme is resolved from the app's
                               tokens to `rgb()`/`rgba()` values xterm can parse (`color-mix()` is
                               refused), and one owned ResizeObserver measures the screen. xterm and
                               its fit and web-links addons are imported dynamically, so no page that shows no
                               terminal carries them. Links in the output open in a new tab, for http(s) only.
                               The pane around that screen is `TerminalPane.vue`, mounted the
                               first time a terminal is asked for (`terminalPanel.ts` carries
                               the one-bit signal) and kept after that, so the screen survives
                               a close. It has two shapes, and the layout picks between them:
                               in the right-hand slot it is the pinned file's own tile
